  - Declaration lines spotlight new products or breaking announcements to capitalize on novelty. Examples: 'Introducing Luxo premium dog kennels', 'NEW: primo chandeliers (just $549 today)', 'Available now: Thrustmaster T-LCM racing pedals (free shipping).' [L0198.006]
  - Apply them to product launches, revived items with upgrades, refreshed versions, new content, videos, or downloadables. [L0198.006]
- **Number:**
  - Numbers in subject lines significantly increase open rates compared with lines without them. Numbers add clarity, precision, and intrigue. Examples: '3 easy steps to choose a surfboard', 'The top 10 induction cooktops 2020', '5 ways that an e-bike will change you.' (older, Source B 2021-22) [L0198.007]
  - Saying 'the top 10' outperforms just 'the top.' (older, Source B 2021-22) [L0198.007]
  - Number formula: state a number and the keyword benefit, for example 'Five budget blenders for smoothies' or 'Three apps that schedule Instagram captions.' It works because a number signals a finite, scannable list and the keyword matches what the reader searched for. [L0380.031]
  - Keep the subject specific to the article it sends readers to. [L0380.031]
- **Curiosity** (older, Source B 2021-22):
  - Curiosity lines withhold information to spark interest. They are structured like questions but are sometimes statements. Examples: 'The little known trick to paddling', 'The big mistake 99% of surfers make', 'You need to see this...' [L0198.008]
  - They succeed because readers worry they are missing knowledge or repeating a common error. [L0198.008]
  - Essential: deliver exactly what the line hints at, or trust and open rates are permanently damaged. [L0198.008]
  - Curiosity formula: a short quoted claim or attributed statement, followed by a secondary line that raises a question or promises an insider detail. [L0569.006]
  - Why it works: an unanswered question creates tension the reader wants to resolve. [L0569.006]
  - Example one: 'The one thing our returns team never tells customers', with preview text 'Ask us this before you buy.' [L0569.006]
  - Example two: 'Why we stopped stocking our bestseller', with preview text 'It wasn't what you think.' [L0569.006]
- **Personalization:**
  - Personalized lines insert the recipient's first name alongside a question or curiosity angle. Examples: 'Jon, did you see this?', 'We've got a big surprise Jon', 'Jon, do you want to save 50%?' (older, Source B 2021-22) [L0198.009]
  - Names stand out because personalization is rare in bulk email. Klaviyo, Drip, and Mailchimp support this with shortcodes that auto-populate names. (older, Source B 2021-22) [L0198.009]
  - Use sparingly to maintain impact. (older, Source B 2021-22) [L0198.009]
  - Personalization formula: open with a first-name token, followed by an offer that reads naturally with the name. It works because a personal opening makes the message feel directed at the reader. [L0547.007]
  - Check that the tool's fallback works for subscribers without a stored name, so no empty gap or raw placeholder appears. [L0547.007]
  - Example in the same pattern: 'Jordan, your spring picks are now on sale'. [L0547.007]
- **Emoji:**
  - Formula: a clear message plus one emoji matched to its meaning, not a string of emojis. [L0569.007]
  - Why it works: the emoji breaks up the visual pattern of plain text lines and signals the type of message before the reader opens it. [L0569.007]
  - Use an alarm clock for time pressure, a lightning bolt for speed, or a flash deal symbol. [L0569.007]
  - Example one: 'Flash sale ends at midnight ⏰', with preview text 'Up to 30 percent off our full range.' [L0569.007]
  - Example two: 'Your restock is here ⚡', with preview text 'Limited quantities, once they are gone they are gone.' [L0569.007]
- **Welcome** (older, Source B 2021-22 is not the source; this is Source C):
  - Keep it short, name the gift or the welcome offer, and avoid heavy sales words so the email reads like a personal message. Examples: 'Your welcome discount is inside' and 'A thank-you gift for joining us.' [L0607.024]
- **Follow-up after a full presentation:**
  - Formula: [specific payoff with an amount] that could [pay or deliver the benefit] [specific next timing]. [L1365.053]
  - Use it for a follow-up email sent after a reader has watched the full presentation. A concrete amount and timing signal a specific offer rather than a generic message. [L1365.053]
  - Why it works: specific numbers and a near-term timeframe create curiosity and make the benefit easy to picture. [L1365.053]
  - Example one: 'The Refund Check That Could Return $480 by Friday'. [L1365.053]
  - Example two: 'The Restock Alert That Could Save You $62 Before Tuesday'. [L1365.053]

### Length

- Keep subject lines as short as possible. They are truncated after a set number of characters on mobile devices, depending on the device and email client. (older, Source B 2021-22) [L0198.010]
- A cut-off subject line shows an incomplete message, so take the time to craft short, punchy subject lines. (older, Source B 2021-22) [L0198.010]

### Tone

- Write subject lines conversationally, as if writing to a friend rather than a crowd. Use informal language and address a single person, not a group, even though many receive the email. (older, Source B 2021-22) [L0198.011]
- At any moment, one person is reading the email. Email is a personal communication tool, so keep that tone. (older, Source B 2021-22) [L0198.011]

### Spam Trigger Words

- Avoid known spam trigger words that inbox providers (Gmail, Hotmail, Yahoo) maintain on spam lists as indicators of promotional or spam emails. Common triggers: money, make money, free, free inside. (older, Source B 2021-22) [L0198.012]
- Some autoresponders check for these words and warn you; others do not. (older, Source B 2021-22) [L0198.012]
- If you use spam words, emails land in spam folders undetected, causing mysteriously low open rates. Manually check your subject lines against spam word lists before sending. (older, Source B 2021-22) [L0198.012]
- Trigger words can send emails to the spam folder or the Gmail promotions tab. Check a current published list of known spam trigger words and keep it in mind while writing each subject line. (older, Source B 2021-22) [L0569.010]
- Trigger words matter less for e-commerce than for other business types, but they still need to be checked. (older, Source B 2021-22) [L0569.010]

### Capitalization and Emoji Tests

- Test different capitalization styles: all lowercase, title case (first letter of each word capitalized), and variations. Testing reveals what stands out most in your audience's inbox. (older, Source B 2021-22) [L0198.013]
- Test emojis in subject lines selectively. They appear in color in inbox lists, which makes them visually stand out and increases the likelihood that recipients notice and read the line. (older, Source B 2021-22) [L0198.014]
- Do not use an emoji on every email. Varying your approach preserves the tactic's effectiveness. (older, Source B 2021-22) [L0198.014]
- Use emojis only when they are genuinely relevant to the email content. (older, Source B 2021-22) [L0198.014]

### Over-Use and Fake Urgency Pitfalls

- Overusing any single subject line tactic, such as questions, scarcity, personalization, or emojis, diminishes its effectiveness. Recipients grow accustomed to it and open rates decline. Rotate among different tactics to maintain impact. (older, Source B 2021-22) [L0198.015]
- Constant daily sends or fake scarcity claims train subscribers to ignore these emails entirely. (older, Source B 2021-22) [L0198.005]
- Faking scarcity destroys audience trust. If you claim limited supply or deadlines that are not genuine, or if these recur constantly, audiences quickly recognize the false pattern and permanently ignore these emails. (older, Source B 2021-22) [L0198.016]
- Overpromising in curiosity or question subject lines erodes trust. If a line says 'The big mistake 99% of surfers make', the email must deliver a significant, widespread error, not a beginner-level or rare mistake. Overhyping without delivery damages credibility for future campaigns. (older, Source B 2021-22) [L0198.017]

### Preview Text

- Email inboxes display a short second line next to the subject line, often called preview text or preheader. Use it as a companion to the subject line, adding a detail, a question, or a hint that supports the subject without repeating it. (older, Source B 2021-22) [L0569.024]
- The subject line and pre-header work as a pair. The subject names the message (a gift, a reminder, a last chance), and the pre-header repeats the offer and the deadline in short form, so the inbox preview shows both the reason to open and the value. [L0606.016]
  - Example 1 for a 10% welcome offer: subject 'Your first order is waiting', pre-header '10% off your first order [valid 48 hours]'. [L0606.016]
  - Example 2 for a saved cart: subject 'Your cart is almost gone', pre-header 'Your saved items plus 10% off [today only]'. [L0606.016]
- Reminder without a discount: playful, self-aware humor about the shopper hesitating, followed by the saved item and one plain reason to return, lowers the pressure of a reminder without a discount. [L0606.043]
  - Example 1: subject 'Your cart is still warm', pre-header 'Your picks are waiting, no pressure.' [L0606.043]
  - Example 2: subject 'Hard to say goodbye to that jacket', pre-header 'The jacket is still in your cart.' [L0606.043]

## Email Copywriting, Design and Templates

### Copy Structure and Purpose

- Email copy has two parts: the subject line and the body copy. Each is written and optimised separately. [L0568.007]
- Marketing emails that aim to get the reader to visit the site need copywriting, because their purpose is persuasion toward a specific action. [L0568.006]
- Direct selling emails start from one core problem of the target market, which the product is positioned to solve. Named frameworks for structuring them include Problem-Agitate-Solution, the Bencivenga persuasion equation and AIDA. [L0475.005]
- The body copy of a marketing email has one job: generate clicks to a destination, not sell inside the inbox (older advice, 2021-22). Persuasion, full proof and purchase decisions belong on the website, where there is room for images, videos and complete copy. [L0602.001]
- If an email tries to make the full purchase case, readers who are not convinced do not click, and the store loses the chance to convert them on the site (older advice, 2021-22). Send traffic straight to the website where the full picture can be shown. [L0602.002]
- Do not answer the question or problem raised in the email (older advice, 2021-22). Tell the reader they will learn the answer through the click, because otherwise there is no reason to take the action. [L0602.023]
- Email shares many similarities with content. Knowing customers' experiences, problems and pain points lets email address them more effectively (older advice, 2021-22). Email marketing built on this research communicates more precisely with the customer. [L0872.008]
- The strategies that work in email are rooted in direct mail marketing and have stayed consistent for decades because human psychology has not changed (older advice, 2021-22). Tested direct mail principles can therefore be applied to email copy and sequencing. [L0609.003]
- Treat every email as a potential reusable template (older advice, 2021-22). Once an email is written, reuse it across other flows instead of writing from scratch, which saves significant time. [L0493.021]
- Message matters more than format. Postage cost and fancy stationery do not increase response; the offer and the opening sentences do. [L1261.157]

### Persuasion Principles

The six power reasons customers buy:

1. **Ownership:** use language and lifestyle images to make them feel they already own it (car salesman technique: give control and choice). [L0261.012]
2. **Logic:** identify the right customer, answer why it is relevant, and confirm it solves their specific problem. [L0261.012]
3. **Social Proof:** include testimonials, reviews, ratings and credible endorsements. [L0261.012]
4. **Scarcity and Urgency:** use a limited-time offer, limited stock language, show others buying, and highlight the cost of inaction. [L0261.012]
5. **Reciprocity:** frame the offer as a gift (free shipping, discount), create obligation, and provide value first. [L0261.012]
6. **Status:** identify the status the customer wants (luxury, eco-friendly, athletic) and position the product to align with and elevate that status. [L0261.012]

### Body Copy Formula and CTA Rules

#### Order of Work

- Decide the call to action before writing the body copy. Typical CTAs in a copy email are learning more about a product, reading a blog post or downloading a lead magnet (older advice, 2021-22). [L0602.004]
- Write the body copy after the subject line, and make it follow on from the subject line's promise. Together they should create curiosity, so the body reveals only enough to make the reader want more (older advice, 2021-22). [L0602.003]

#### Body Copy Formula

Email body formula for e-commerce copy emails, in order (older advice, 2021-22):

1. Greet the reader and confirm the problem that the CTA will help them solve.
2. Tease the solution, which is the product's primary promise or the answer the content provides.
3. Explain at least one benefit of taking the CTA.
4. Offer the CTA.
5. Sign off.

[L0602.010]

- After the subject line, briefly name the primary value or benefit the reader gets by taking the CTA. Do not explain the whole content in the email (older advice, 2021-22). [L0602.007]
- For a content CTA, one primary benefit is usually enough. If there are several, list up to three primary benefits and keep each one short, because the content itself delivers the detail (older advice, 2021-22). [L0602.008]
- For a product CTA, focus the email on the product's primary promise. If the product copy already has a clear primary promise, reuse that promise in the email (older advice, 2021-22). [L0602.009]
- Open the email body with a short greeting that also introduces the sender, so the reader knows who is writing before the problem is named (older advice, 2021-22). [L0602.025]
- Confirm the problem by pointing to how often customers ask about it, for example by saying the store receives many emails or questions on the topic. This makes the problem feel recognised and real before the solution is teased (older advice, 2021-22). [L0602.027]
- Name the destination in the email, such as a blog post, guide, product page or download. This avoids surprising readers when the page asks for something other than a simple read, especially when the link leads to an opt-in download (older advice, 2021-22). [L0602.026]

#### Length

- A copy email for e-commerce should be a few hundred words in four or five short paragraphs (older advice, 2021-22). Longer formats are used by digital product, consulting and coaching businesses, not typical e-commerce stores that send traffic to a full site. [L0602.011]
- A complete copy email that follows the problem, solution, benefit and CTA formula, with two benefits and one guide link, can run about 145 words (older advice, 2021-22). [L0602.029]

#### Body Copy Checklist

Body copy format checklist, in order (older advice, 2021-22):

1. Use a minimum font size of 14 point or larger.
2. Use short, simple sentences in easy-to-read language.
3. Use short paragraphs of one to two lines, and single-line paragraphs are common.
4. Make the CTA or links stand out.
5. Use only one CTA per email.
6. Use a personalised signature.

[L0602.012]

- Sign emails with a real person's name, either the owner or a team member, rather than a generic business name or the word Team. Emails that feel personal from a person to a person tend to get better response rates (older advice, 2021-22). [L0602.015]

#### One CTA, Placed in Several Spots

- Focus most emails on exactly one call to action. Mixing several offers or links in one email reduces the response to each of them. Several different CTAs overwhelm readers, lower interaction and often lead them to ignore the email and not return to it (older advice, 2021-22). [L0569.014, L0602.005]
- Adding many calls to action to one email reduces the number of clicks for each one. Avoid daily-deal style emails stuffed with many offers unless that is actually your business model (older advice, 2021-22). [L0569.015]
- Several clickable elements in one email are acceptable if they all point to the same CTA and the same content. Multiple different CTAs to different content should be avoided (older advice, 2021-22). [L0602.022]
- Place the one CTA in several spots that all lead to the same page: a link within the sentence that names the topic, a clear click instruction in the middle, and a link or button near the end. These are multiple clickable areas for a single action, not several different offers (older advice, 2021-22). [L0602.028]

#### Button or Text Link

- Use a button rather than a plain text link for the main call to action. Research cited in the lesson indicates that buttons improve click-through rates and action (older advice, 2021-22). [L0569.012]
- A CTA can be a clickable button or a standard underlined text link in a different colour, such as "click here to view". Test both styles, since neither is proven to be better in general (older advice, 2021-22). [L0602.014]
- The two positions differ. The button is the recommended default, and the text link is an accepted alternative. When you cannot tell which works better for your list, run both as a test. [L0569.012, L0602.014]
- The call-to-action button must use a unique colour with strong contrast against the rest of the email, such as bright red, bright green or bright blue. Any colour works as long as the button clearly stands out (older advice, 2021-22). [L0569.013]

#### Pattern: Problem, Guide, Two Benefits, One CTA

Pattern (older advice, 2021-22):

- A curiosity-driven subject line.
- A greeting.
- One sentence that names a common buying problem.
- A sentence saying there is confusing information online.
- A sentence promising a guide or resource that settles the question.
- Two benefits the reader gets from clicking.
- One repeated CTA with a clear link or button.

Why it works: the reader sees a problem they recognise, a promise of an answer and a single clear next step, so the click is the only logical action. [L0602.024]

- Example A (running shoes, subject: "Flat feet, which shoe?"): "Hi Sam, choosing running shoes with flat feet is a big decision. Advice online contradicts itself on stability versus cushioning, so we wrote a short guide. Click here to see which features matter most and which model our fitters recommend for your stride." [L0602.024]
- Example B (mattresses, subject: "Firm or soft, which is right for your back?"): "Hi Lee, picking a mattress for back pain is not something to rush. There are many mixed opinions about firm and soft, so we put together a simple comparison. Click here to see the pros and cons of each and which one our sleep team suggests for back sleepers." [L0602.024]

#### Pairing a Discount With a Product

- Pair the discount with one specific product suggestion in a single sentence that links the saving to a second purchase. This turns the coupon into a reason to add to the basket rather than just a price cut. [L0606.044]
  - Example 1: "Your 15% covers a second pair of socks, so add the matching cap while you are here." [L0606.044]
  - Example 2: "Use your 15% on the refill pack and the saving pays for a travel size of the same cream." [L0606.044]

### Writing Process and Scannable Style

- Keep emails easy to scan with one or two sentences per line, lots of white space and no long paragraphs. Readers scan first and read only what hooks them. Short lines keep attention on the story and offer. [L0523.122]
- Use short, choppy sentences and short paragraphs of one to two lines (older advice, 2021-22). [L0569.011]
- Writing process for emails: [L0523.123]
  1. Write the basic structure of the email first, including the story beats and the offer link.
  2. Add the juicy details, emotional hooks and personality afterwards.
  3. Dictate ideas into a phone recorder and send the recording to someone for transcription if writing is slow.
- Email body text for flows can be drafted with ChatGPT, but the draft still needs checking. Before going live, confirm the links point to the correct checkout and product pages and that the wording matches the offer. [L0476.040]
- Small grammar errors are acceptable in emails and can make the character more human and relatable. Do not delay sending because of fear of mistakes, since consistent sending matters more than perfect text. The flaw is part of the bond. [L0523.124]
- Keep a separate swipe file of emails collected from competitors and from marketers in other niches. Use it as a source of ideas when you lack inspiration for your own email content, without copying it directly (older advice, 2021-22). [L0609.018]

### Font Size and Readability

- Set the email body font to at least 14 point. Automatic sizes of 12 or 10 point that many email tools produce are too small and hard to read for some people (older advice, 2021-22). [L0602.013]
- Body copy should use a minimum font size of 18 pt, with up to 22 pt acceptable, because bigger text is generally easier to read (older advice, 2021-22). [L0569.011]
- The two figures conflict. Use 18-22 pt for body copy where you can, and treat 14 pt as the floor for copy emails. [L0569.011, L0602.013]

### Personalisation and Dynamic Content

- Email content can use personalisation variables such as {{firstName}} with a fallback value like 'Friend' for customers without a stored first name. [L0282.012]
- Personalise the greeting and subject line with the first name. Adding the subscriber's first name makes the email feel more personal. [L0607.025]
- Add a default such as 'there' to every personalised greeting and subject line. Otherwise an empty first name in the profile leaves an empty greeting. [L0606.022]
- Personalisation in Klaviyo (Liquid) for the greeting and the subject line:

```text
Greeting: Hey {{ first_name|default:'there' }},
Cart subject line: Still thinking it over, {{ first_name|default:'there'|title }}?

Use the fallback in both places, so an empty first name in the profile does not show up as a blank greeting.
```
[L0606.008]

- The two fallback values differ. 'Friend' is the general personalisation fallback for variables (L0282.012). 'there' is the greeting and subject fallback used in the Klaviyo flow templates (L0606.008, L0606.022). Use 'Friend' for general content variables and 'there' where the greeting reads "Hey there,". [L0282.012, L0606.008, L0606.022]
- The placeholder syntax also differs between sources. Klaviyo uses {{ first_name }} (L0606.025 shows it without a fallback, although L0606.022 requires one). Generic text uses {first_name} and {discount_code}, which you replace yourself (L0607.023). [L0606.025, L0606.022, L0607.023]
- Placeholder mapping in the flow templates: [L0606.007]
  - {{ first_name }} and [NAME TAG] take the subscriber's first name from the Klaviyo profile.
  - [DISCOUNT CODE] takes the discount code of that flow.
  - [BUTTON] is a call-to-action button linking to the store.
  - [CART] is the block listing the products left in the abandoned cart.
  - [BEST SELLERS] is a product block of top sellers.
  - [REVIEWS] is a block of customer reviews.
  - [TIMER] is a countdown.
  - [SIGNATURE] is the sender sign-off.
- Dynamic content may not appear fully in email previews, so check the preview of each campaign before sending. [L0864.020]

### Subject Lines and Pre-Headers

The flow subject lines and pre-headers, from the Source C table: [L0606.009]

| Flow | Email | Subject line | Pre-header | Offer | Deadline in copy |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Welcome | 1 | A Welcome Gift For You | 10% off storewide [last 3 days] | 10% storewide | Discount vanishes in three days |
| Welcome | 2 | Passing over our welcome gift? | 10% off storewide [48 hours left] | 10% storewide | Valid until tomorrow |
| Welcome | 3 | Last Day to redeem your welcome gift | 10% off storewide [24 hours left] | 10% storewide | Vanishes in a few hours, last day |
| Cart abandoned | 1 | Still thinking it over, [first name in title case]? | Looks like you left your shopping bag behind. | No discount, 100% satisfaction guarantee mentioned | None |
| Cart abandoned | 2 | Here's our 'miss you' gift... | 10% off on your saved cart. | 10% on the saved cart | Very limited time |
| Cart abandoned | 3 | Your discounted cart is about to expire | Your discounted cart is about to expire | 10% on the saved cart | Cart expires and offer ends very soon |
| Cart abandoned | 4 | We have to give it to someone else | It could be a photo-finish to grab your discounted cart (last few hours) | 10% on the saved cart | Last few hours |
| Post-purchase | 1 | Our Thank You Gift For You | 15% off storewide [Last 3 days] | 15% on the next cart | Last 3 days in pre-header |
| Post-purchase | 2 | Last 2 Days | 15% off. You're missing out! [Last 48 hrs] | 15% on the next cart | Last 48 hours |
| Post-purchase | 3 | We Hope You Make It | 15% off storewide [Last 24 hours] | 15% on the next cart | Expires today, offer ends at midnight |
| Thank-you | 1 | Planning to spoil us? | Thanks for the vote of trust! | No discount, VIP perks listed | None |

- The subject line in the cart-abandoned template L0606.028 uses {{ first_name |title }} without a fallback, while the table and L0606.008 specify title case with 'there' as the fallback. Use the fallback version. [L0606.028, L0606.008, L0606.009]

#### Flow Setup and Review Block

Setting up the flows in Klaviyo: [L0606.006]

1. Create or open the Klaviyo account.
2. Create the abandoned cart flow, the welcome flow and the post-purchase flow.
3. Load the ready-made team template for each flow into the matching flow, one email per step, in the order given below.
4. Replace every blank (______) and every bracketed placeholder with real store content, your real discount code and your real links.
5. Check the offer, the deadline wording and the review block in each email before turning the flow on.

- Place the review block at the end of each email, after the call to action. Reviews act as social proof and reinforce the decision without competing with the discount code. [L0606.024]
- Keep the stock line only if it is accurate, and keep the 100% satisfaction line only if the store offers that guarantee. [L0606.028, L0606.033]
- Every flow email should show the brand logo and footer, which Klaviyo takes from the brand assets once they are set up. A footer can also hold social media icons, which are added as their own section in the email builder. [L0607.053]

### Claims, Accuracy and Launch Checks

- Vague superlatives and invented numbers such as "150% more awesome" or a "100% satisfaction guarantee" must not stay in the copy unless they are real. Replace them with a specific, verifiable benefit, and only mention a guarantee the store actually offers in its return policy. [L0606.020]
- Leaving blanks (______), [NAME TAG], [BUTTON], [REVIEWS] or [DISCOUNT CODE] unreplaced before launch sends visible placeholder text to customers. Check a test send of every email in the flow before switching it on. [L0606.021]
- An email inside a flow that is still in draft status is not sent, so an unwanted email can be kept safe by leaving it in draft. [L1297.044]
- Before using a bought email template, check that it works on mobile and across all email clients. Look-alike designs can fail in real inboxes even when they look good on a marketplace page. [L1263.006]

### Plain Text vs HTML

#### Positions in the Sources

| Position | Source | When it applies |
|---|---|---|
| HTML is preferred over plain text, though the gap matters less than it used to. HTML allows clickable buttons and a logo, so use it, but keep the layout simple (older advice, 2021-22). | L0569.001 | When you need buttons and a logo. |
| Simple text-based emails are preferred for small and medium e-commerce stores. HTML remains useful when images must be shown (older advice, 2021-22). | L0602.016 | Small and medium stores; HTML when images must be shown. |
| Write emails mostly as plain text with few or no images. The wording sells, and many email clients do not display images. | L0572.018 | Default writing mode. |
| Plain text emails that look like a message from a friend can perform well, even compared with heavily designed templates. | L0923.037 | Any list used for many offers. |
| For a new store, start with text-led emails that use a few images, since they are more likely to reach the primary inbox. Compare open and click results in a test rather than relying on taste. | L0607.052 | New stores; test to decide. |

- Heavily designed HTML emails are typically associated with promotional mailings and are not how people usually write to each other (older advice, 2021-22). [L0602.016]
- Text-based emails with a few images are more likely to land in the primary inbox. Image-heavy emails from the start risk the promotions tab, where they are opened less. [L0607.025]
- Heavily formatted HTML emails may not display well across all inboxes, devices and email clients. Some evidence suggests they are more likely to be classed as promotional and land in the Promotions tab in Gmail or in spam (older advice, 2021-22). [L0602.017]
- Large big-box e-commerce stores often send three or four heavily formatted HTML emails per day with many offers. The author does not consider this the best approach for small to medium e-commerce businesses (older advice, 2021-22). [L0602.018]
- Elaborate HTML design is unnecessary for a simple email with one CTA. There is no evidence that heavily formatted emails achieve higher click-through rates, and some evidence points the other way (older advice, 2021-22). [L0602.019]
- Overloading HTML emails with decorative elements, many images and many colours is a mistake (older advice, 2021-22). Email providers may strip out part of the code, and the layout can break on some devices, so the email may look broken or make less sense. [L0569.002]
- Build email layouts with HTML and CSS instead of image banners. Images can be blocked, so the layout should work without them. [L0572.019]
- Include links to a few website categories in most emails. Recipients who are not interested in the email's topic may still click through to the shop. [L0572.020]
- Email HTML is not web HTML. It is still largely stuck in 1990s-era techniques and renders differently in each email host, for example Gmail, Yahoo and Hotmail render the same design differently. [L1263.007]
- Design and code emails for the lowest common denominator across all email clients. Learn what each client supports and does not support before building. [L1263.008]

### Images, Colour and Visual Layout

- Use images sparingly and choose only the best product images, such as the product itself or the product in use. Many images raise the risk of spam-folder placement and of formatting being stripped, and heavy HTML can look like scam emails. Product emails benefit from one product image, while many email types need no images at all (older advice, 2021-22). [L0569.018]
- There is no reliable evidence that emails containing images perform better than text-only emails. Add images only when they serve the message, such as a product photo, and choose your best product images rather than decorative graphics (older advice, 2021-22). [L0569.025]
- A combined image of a picture and short text, such as a collage of customer testimonial screenshots, can be made in Canva and placed as an image block in a Klaviyo email. Used in abandoned cart emails, this adds social proof without making the whole email an image. [L0573.037]
- Include pinnable product images in emails so subscribers can save products to Pinterest when they are not ready to buy. This keeps the product visible and gives the customer a place to return to later. [L1136.032]
- Put the text on a white background so it is easy to read. A column of text with a different coloured background on either side is acceptable as long as the area behind the text stays white (older advice, 2021-22). [L0569.017]
- In a campaign email, white is a clean base for product-focused emails. The background colour is set in the editor's styling options, and offers, text blocks and product feeds are added as extra components on top of this background. [L0573.036]
- Limit email colours to your brand colours, usually one or two primary colours already used on the website. Use these colours when you need to add colour to an email (older advice, 2021-22). [L0569.019]
- Apply one consistent email template to all emails so that subscribers recognise every message as coming from your brand immediately (older advice, 2021-22). [L0569.020]
- Consistent design builds on trust that already exists with subscribers, while changing styles and layouts from email to email creates doubt about who is sending. Keep the design recognisable across all emails (older advice, 2021-22). [L0569.021]
- Email design should stay simple and not be overthought. Most results come from strong copy, a clear message and a call to action that is easy to identify (older advice, 2021-22). [L0569.022]

### Klaviyo Editor Structure

- The Klaviyo email editor builds content from sections and blocks, like Shopify theme editing. A button block cannot sit between two blocks of text directly. Add two text blocks and insert the button between them. [L0573.020, L0607.026]
- In a campaign, add a product feed: click Product > Create product feed, then show a collection, best-selling products or most viewed products over a set period such as 90 days. This promotes the main products during an event. [L0573.029, L0607.046]
- The dynamic product section works the same way. Under Products, click Create product feed, then choose to show best-selling or most viewed products over a set time range, for example 90 days. [L0607.046, L0573.029]

### Modular Templates and Content Blocks

#### Building Blocks

- Email modules work like building blocks. Each one can be reused and stacked in any order inside an email. The same modular approach applies to both campaigns and flows. [L0610.001]
- Content blocks and modules are the individual parts of an email, while a template is the complete email assembled from them. Using one shared set of content blocks and templates keeps campaigns and flows consistent. [L0610.002]
- Build a system of reusable modular content blocks rather than one individually designed email. The same blocks can then be used for every campaign and every flow you send in the future. [L1263.016]
- Some modules, such as M19, are universal and nearly every brand can use them. Those are the first modules to build in a library. [L0610.008]
- With a ready module library, done-for-you clients can build an email in three to five minutes instead of designing each email from scratch. [L0610.009]
- All modules are tested on mobile and in dark mode, so emails built from them render correctly without extra checking. Building from tested blocks lets you produce emails quickly with consistent results. [L0610.010]
- The always-on programme covers both campaigns and automated flows and should remain editable in the email platform. Sliced-image designs look attractive but cannot scale into flows and break in real inboxes. [L1263.019]

#### Module Library

Email module library: 21 reusable modules, 8 system modules (sage) and 13 content modules (terracotta). Most emails are remixes of these modules. [L0610.005]

| Category | Modules |
|---|---|
| Header and footer | Header (no nav), Header (with nav), Footer (no nav), Footer (with nav) |
| Hero and imagery | Image Hero, Big Number Hero, Announcement Bar |
| Product | Product Left, Product Right, 2-up Product Grid, 3-up Product Grid |
| Conversion and trust | Primary CTA, Promo Code Callout, USP Strip, Social Proof, Stats Row |
| Text and structure | Long form Text, Quote, Founder Sign-off, Section Divider, Thanks founder name |
| Coded modules | M5A, M5B, M8A, M8B, M9, M11, M12, M13, M14, M15, M16, M17, M18, M19 |

- The Promo Code Callout module in the example library states the full offer on one line: a percentage discount, its scope and the code to enter, such as "20% off everything with code SAVE20". Putting percentage, scope and code together lets the reader see the whole offer in one block. [L0610.013]

#### Standard Layouts

Editorial email template, in order: [L0610.003]

1. Header
2. M11 module
3. A second M11 module with founder sign-off
4. M12 module
5. Footer

Save the finished structure as an editorial template and clone it each time an editorial email is needed.

Product showcase email template, in order: [L0610.004]

1. Hero section (large image or big number hero)
2. Product Left
3. Product Right
4. Three-up product grid, with two of the products stacked on top of each other
5. Footer

#### Creating a Custom Module

Creating a custom email module when no existing module fits: [L0610.007]

1. Describe the specific use case to Claude.
2. Have Claude Design build the new module for that use case.
3. Upload the finished module to the module library.

#### Reusing a Design Across a Flow

Reusing one Klaviyo email design across a flow: [L0477.038]

1. Build the first email from a pre-made template, editing the mobile version first.
2. Click the arrow next to edit email, choose save as template and name it after your store.
3. For each further email, click the arrow next to edit email, choose change template, drag and drop, open My templates, select the saved store template and click Use template.
4. Replace the text with your prepared copy and set the email from draft to live.

### Design Method Comparison

- Email design methods are compared on two axes: programme fit (editability, render safety and scalability, higher is better) and design wow factor (higher to the right). Every method involves trade-offs, so no single option wins on all axes. [L1263.001]
- The same method comparison applies to other email platforms such as Omnisend. The tool name changes, but the trade-offs between editability, render safety and design polish stay the same. [L1263.014]

| Method | Strength | Weakness |
|---|---|---|
| Self-design in Canva or Figma | Full control if you have real graphic and email design skill | Easily looks amateur, can damage the brand, often abandoned after weeks or months |
| Bought template (Envato, ThemeForest, similar marketplaces) | Quick start, can look good on paper | Often made by graphic designers, may be image-based, mobile and cross-client rendering often unverified |
| Third-party tool (BeFree, later renamed LGE Studio) | Polished, modular templates | Two-tool workflow, pushed to Klaviyo as one large HTML block, every edit needs a re-push |
| Professional designer file (Figma or Canva) | Pleasing, professional look | Delivered as locked images, text can be changed but layout and module order cannot, does not scale into flows |
| Klaviyo native editor | Editable, render-safe, repeatable | Needs patience and skill to produce polished design |
| Klaviyo Composer (alpha at time of lesson) | Expected to allow better native, editable Klaviyo designs | Still in alpha, full extent unknown |
| Modular system with reusable content blocks | On-brand, editable, scalable for campaigns and flows | Less polished than a bespoke designer look |

[L1263.002]

- Only self-design emails in Canva or Figma if you have professional graphic design and email design skills. If you lack them, avoid this route entirely. [L1263.003]
- Graphic design and email design are different skills. Email design depends on specific psychology and on the placement of modules, which a graphic designer may not know. [L1263.004]
- Founders who design their own emails without the right skills often spend weeks or months on it and then give up for another route. Self-designed emails can also do real damage to the brand. [L1263.005]
- BeFree, later rebranded as LGE Studio, is a third-party tool for building modular email templates and pushing them to Klaviyo. The pushed result is one large HTML block, so every change requires returning to the tool and pushing again. [L1263.010]
- Professional designer files in Figma or Canva are usually locked images. Buyers often can change text but cannot change the layout, and the building blocks do not stack in any order. [L1263.011]
- The Klaviyo native editor produces editable, render-safe emails. It requires patience and design skill to make the result look polished. [L1263.012]
- Klaviyo Composer was in alpha and expected to be released publicly within about one month of the lesson. It is meant to help design better emails natively in Klaviyo while staying editable and scalable. [L1263.013]
- The modular system trades some visual polish for on-brand consistency, editability and scalability. It suits operators who send many emails and value speed over a bespoke look. [L1263.015]
- The modular email system described in this method is also a two-tool workflow, similar to a third-party tool setup. Its main advantage is reusable content blocks and campaign-level scaling, not a single-tool process. [L1263.021]
- The modular email system is positioned as the most widely rendered option among the methods compared, and it should work on mobile and in dark mode. It is chosen for scalability first, with polish as a secondary consideration. [L1263.022]
- On the programme-fit scale (editability, render safety, scalability), a modular system built with an AI approach and Klaviyo's native editor score roughly equal. The sliced-image designer route may look more impressive, but it breaks in real inboxes and cannot scale into flows. [L1263.020]
- Choose a professional designer if the priority is a fully bespoke look and you accept locked, non-editable files. Choose the modular system if the priority is editability, consistent branding and scaling across campaigns and flows. [L1263.018]

### Brand Voice File

- Markdown is the most AI-friendly format for storing brand voice and style rules because it is easy for language models to parse, structure and reference when generating brand-specific content. [L0318.011]
- Brand voice template, to be filled in for each brand:

```markdown
# [Brand Name], Brand Voice

## Tone and personality description
Write 1-2 sentences that capture the brand's communication style, personality, and the emotional frame it uses to present its category. Example: "Silk The Label speaks with a calm, aspirational tone that frames sleep as a luxurious act of self-care-blending functional product confidence with gentle, wellness-driven warmth."

## Core voice attributes (integrate consistently)
- [Attribute 1]
- [Attribute 2]
- [Attribute 3]
- [Attribute 4]
- [Attribute 5]

## Prohibited voice patterns (enforce as non-negotiable rules)
- [What to avoid, e.g., "Overly clinical or technical language"]
- [What to avoid, e.g., "Loud, pushy sales tactics"]
- [What to avoid, e.g., "Casual or flippant tone that undermines the luxury feel"]

## Aesthetic and positioning framework
Describe the sensory and linguistic character, the design philosophy, and the market position the brand occupies. Example: "Silk The Label presents a refined, feminine luxury aesthetic built around soft jewel tones, clean typography, and aspirational lifestyle imagery."

Aesthetic markers: [descriptor words, e.g., "luxurious, minimal, feminine, elegant, serene"]

## Proven performance samples (accumulate over time)
<!-- Collect subject lines and openers that drive the strongest results as you run campaigns -->
```
[L0318.010]

- After generating your voice file, use the Examples section to record actual subject lines and email openers that performed well in your campaigns. Add new examples as you launch each campaign, building a live reference library of how your brand voice translates to practice. This prevents drift into generic territory and gives your AI-generated content proven patterns to match. [L0318.020]

### AI-Assisted Email Building

- Ask the AI first for the best prompt for a writing task, then use that prompt for the actual copy. This two-step approach is meant to produce stronger output than a single generic request. [L0620.002]
- Campaign calendars let you generate a series of emails covering weeks or months in one or a few prompts. This saves a large amount of time compared with building each email by hand in Klaviyo. [L1263.017]
- A modular system built with an AI approach is one of the options that scores well on programme fit. [L1263.020]
- Flow body text drafted with ChatGPT still needs checking against the links and the offer before launch. [L0476.040]

### Launch Procedure for Flows

1. Create the flow and load the matching template for each email, in the order given. [L0606.006]
2. Replace every blank and bracketed placeholder with real store content, the real discount code and real links. [L0606.006, L0606.021]
3. Check a test send of every email in the flow. [L0606.021]
4. Check the offer, the deadline wording and the review block in each email. [L0606.006]
5. Turn the flow on only when all emails pass. [L0606.006, L0606.021]

### Email body copy and calls to action

- Older advice (2021-22): an email has one job, which is to get the reader to visit the website. Trying to close the sale inside the message misses that job, because the purchase decision belongs to the site. [L1460.005]
- Length matters less than whether each paragraph keeps the reader's interest. Long copy can outperform short copy when the reader is already interested, because he will read several pages to learn more. [L1369.097]
- A short promotional email that produced a single tracked result used this structure [L1356.072]:
  1. The headline is used as the subject line.
  2. Two or three one-line payoff sentences follow.
  3. One big promise.
  4. One quote from an authority figure.
  5. One link, as the only call to action.
  
  The email revealed no product details, so readers had to click to learn more. In that tracked campaign, roughly 11 out of every 100 recipients clicked. This is a single result to watch, not a guaranteed benchmark. [L1356.072]
- Place social proof directly beside each unique value proposition, such as a review, a bestseller or a customer story. Claims alone persuade less than claims shown with evidence. [L1470.017]
- Remove text that repeats information already in the email header, such as a brand name next to a logo. This keeps the email shorter and easier to scan. [L1470.015]
- Compose.ai is a tool for writing marketing emails, useful when a store needs many email drafts without hiring a copywriter. [L1416.009]

### Brand colors in the email

- Keep the email text and background colors black and white, and use only the brand color for the button fill, so the emails match the store's look without being distracting. [L1317.006]
- The Chrome extension "ColorPick Eyedropper" (named "color picker" in the lesson) copies the six-digit hex code of any color on a page to the clipboard, so the exact brand color can be pasted into Shopify's color fields. [L1317.007]

### Brand voice and project files

- Keep the brand kit, tone-of-voice files, templates and campaign work in one permanent project that the AI can always access. Files in a one-off chat are lost when the chat closes, and then the AI guesses at the brand instead of using the real files. [L1506.001]
- Build the work in a fixed order: brand kit and tone of voice first, then the email templates and universal content blocks built from them, then campaigns built from both. Each step reads the output of the previous one, so every file must sit in the same place for this hand-off to work. [L1506.002]
- A tidy project folder for this email workflow uses numbered folders for each stage, such as workspace setup, brand context, template from scratch and building campaigns, plus a stack setup folder and an archive folder for older files. [L1506.030]

### Claude Cowork workspace setup

Workspace setup for Claude Cowork and Klaviyo [L1506.006]:

1. Download the Claude desktop app from claude.com and install it like any other program.
2. Open the app, which starts on the Chat tab, and switch to Cowork.
3. Go to Projects and click New project, then choose to start from scratch.
4. Name the project, for example Email Template Project, and optionally add a short description such as building email templates to push to Klaviyo. No detailed instructions are needed.
5. Choose the project location folder and click Create.
6. Confirm that Memory is switched on for the project.
7. Select the model (Opus is used in this setup) and choose the approval mode (see the approval setting).
8. Create a dedicated folder inside the project location and put all downloaded course files into it.
9. Always start new chats from inside the project, never from a standalone chat or a new Cowork task.

- Creating a project: in Cowork, go to Projects, click New project and choose to start from scratch. Give it a name and a short description, then choose the project location folder and click Create. The Memory toggle is shown in the same dialog and must be on. [L1506.007]
- The project instructions field is optional. A short line of context describing the goal, such as building email templates to push to Klaviyo, is enough to start. [L1506.008]
- Project memory must be switched on so Claude keeps the conversation history across all chats in the same project. Without it, the brand and background must be re-explained in every new chat. [L1506.009]
- In a project, the page shows an Instructions section, a Context section where files are added with the plus button, and a Memory section where you can ask Claude to remember something. All chats started in the project appear there with their history. [L1506.010]
- The workspace setup for AI-written Klaviyo email templates takes about five minutes and is done once. If it is skipped, the AI loses access to the brand kit and image files and starts guessing at the brand. [L1506.026]

### Installing and using skills

Installing a skill [L1506.021]:

1. Go to Customize > Skills.
2. Click the plus button, choose Create skill, then Upload skill.
3. Drag the skill files from the course modules into the upload window.
4. Wait a few seconds until the skill appears as installed and ready to use.

- To invoke a skill in Cowork, type the slash character (/) in the chat input. A list of installed skills and the file picker appears, and selecting a skill and pressing Enter starts it without any extra prompt. [L1506.022]
- The email builder skill creates email templates that are pushed to Klaviyo as editable templates. The campaign studio skill creates campaigns directly from within Cowork, and the output appears in the Klaviyo account. [L1506.023]

### Modular content blocks and master template

- Universal content blocks are reusable email building blocks. A set of them with a master template lets the same modules power welcome flows, campaigns and one-off sends, and templates built this way stay consistent across all sends. [L1361.011]
- Universal content blocks are created with the email template builder skill from two reference images, an anatomy image and a module library image, together with brand-kit.json. The output is a set of blocks and a master template pushed into Klaviyo. [L1361.021]
- Once universal content blocks exist in Klaviyo, new emails are built quickly by swapping images and reordering a few modules, which keeps every send consistent in look. [L1361.015]
- After the foundation stage (universal content blocks and master template), a brand can already stop and use working templates for sales postcards, product showcases and editorial emails, because later stages only add flows, audits and segmentation. [L1361.014]
- Do not push an AI-written email into Klaviyo as one large raw HTML block, because the result cannot be edited in Klaviyo and must be re-exported to change it. Push templates and universal content blocks as structures that stay editable in Klaviyo. [L1506.017]

### AI-assisted build sequence

Planned sequence of the AI email build [L1361.013]:

1. Set up the tech stack and install all tools (Claude Cowork, Klaviyo MCP, Brandfetch MCP, optional image tools).
2. Create a brand context document from the brand kit (brand-kit.json with voice, colors and fonts).
3. Build the universal content blocks and master template with the email template builder skill, then push them into Klaviyo.
4. Build flows such as the welcome flow using the same blocks, then map the flows.
5. Run an email deliverability audit across Gmail, Hotmail and Yahoo accounts with DNS access to the brand domain.
6. Audit Klaviyo send data and build audience segments.
7. Create a campaign calendar with the email strategy planning skill and feed it into Klaviyo.
8. Optionally add SMS opt-in flows and copywriting.

- The AI-to-email stack changes very fast, so specific tools and workflows must be re-checked regularly. Klaviyo is releasing its own AI composer tools that will change the workflow. [L1361.016]
- Campaigns created through the campaign skill are saved in Klaviyo as drafts, not as live campaigns, so they still need to be reviewed and scheduled inside Klaviyo. [L1506.027]

### Welcome flow

- Welcome flow design uses Excalidraw for flow maps, Claude Cowork with the Klaviyo MCP, and the email flow builder skill, with brand-kit.json and the master template as inputs. The target result is one live welcome flow and four flow maps. [L1361.022]

### Rendering and testing

- Email templates must render well in major email clients such as Gmail, Yahoo and Hotmail, on mobile devices and in dark mode. Test each template across these before using it in production. [L1361.012]

### Hero image, layout and links in Klaviyo

- Use a welcome email hero image of 1250 x 2335 px. Check the file quality after downloading and before uploading, since blurry images reduce trust. [L1470.013]
- Check a hero image's dimensions and file size in the file properties before uploading it. The example welcome hero measured 1250 x 2335 px at 309 KB and looked sharp at that size. [L1470.030]
- Set the top padding of the email content block to 30 px so the first element does not sit against the top edge. A text block that is not full width on mobile is acceptable for this layout. [L1470.014]
- In Klaviyo's email builder, select the hero image block, choose Upload image, and drag the file in to replace the old one. The Image block settings include Link address, Image size with Width set to auto, Fill column on mobile, Alignment, Background color, Border, and Padding. Preview the email before saving to confirm the image and padding render correctly on desktop and mobile. [L1470.029]
- To link an email or SMS button to a bestseller page on most Shopify stores, open storedomain.com/collections to see the store's pre-built collection list. Open the bestseller collection there and copy its URL into the link field. [L1470.028]
- In the greeting, insert the first_name profile property with a default fallback so subscribers without a stored name still get a natural opening line. Open Preview profile info to check how the greeting renders with a real subscriber profile before going live. [L1470.031]

#### Further details

- To prevent image distortion in Shopify email templates on mobile: 1. Click the Mobile Preview tab while editing the email. 2. Check how images render on mobile. 3. Select each image and add padding, typically 10 pixels on each side. 4. This provides space between images and the email borders. [L0278.034]

- A template should be confirmed to work on mobile, in dark mode and across all major email clients before you use it. Image-only emails are a poor choice if you need scalability and programmability. [L1263.009]

## Campaign Cadence, Content Mix and Weekly Email Programs

### Sending Frequency by Lifecycle Phase

**Automated sequences for new subscribers** (Source B, 2021-22, older advice)

- There is no fixed industry rule for email frequency. Use a hybrid approach: send many emails when a subscriber is new and very interested, then reduce the volume as their interest cools. [L0603.001]
- A new subscriber's interest peaks right after they join or make a first purchase. That window is the best time to sell or sell again. Interest then declines, so the attention and offers needed to keep the person engaged shrink. [L0603.002]
- On joining, the email goals are to build trust, establish authority in the niche, and drive a first sale, an upsell, or a repeat purchase. Once trust and authority are built, far less effort is needed to maintain them. [L0603.003]
- Run each phase as its own automated sequence. [L0603.004]

| Phase | Timing | Automated emails per week | Total in phase |
|---|---|---|---|
| Hot phase | Days 0-30 | 4 | 16 |
| Cooling phase | Days 30-60 | 2-3 | - |
| Long-term phase | Day 60 onward | 1 | - |

[L0603.004]

- **Hot phase (days 0-30):** Send 4 automated emails per week, 16 in total. Engagement typically drops after day 30, so the chance of a sale falls for a while if nothing is sent or bought. [L0603.005]
- **Cooling phase (days 30-60):** Reduce to 2 or 3 automated emails per week, using a separate sequence for this phase. [L0603.006]
- **Long-term phase (day 60 onward):** Send 1 automated email per week. [L0603.006]
- In the first 30 days, a subscriber receives 5 emails per week in total: 4 automated emails plus 1 blast email. [L0603.008]
- While a subscriber is in the hot phase, more offers can be placed in front of them because they are most receptive. As interest cools, offer frequency should drop along with overall email volume. [L0603.024]

**Blast emails**

- Blast emails (one-off sends for a new blog post, news, a new product or collection, or a sale such as Black Friday) are separate from automated emails. [L0603.007]
- On average, send about 1 blast email per week. Send more during big sale events and fewer in quiet periods. [L0603.007]
- Blasts usually go to the whole list regardless of lifecycle stage, unless they are aimed at a specific segment. [L0603.008]

**Touch points and persistence** (Source B, 2021-22, older advice)

- Plan for about 7 touch points before a typical person buys, so more emails create more chances to sell. Not every email has to be a sales email, but each should be a chance to sell or to deliver value that leads toward a sale. [L0603.009]
- Most sales happen after the 5th, 6th, or 7th follow-up rather than the first few contacts, so people often need to see a brand several times before buying. If the emails provide relevant value, err on the side of sending more rather than fewer. [L0603.010]
- Brands that consistently have genuinely useful content can email subscribers every day without harm, as long as unsubscribe and open-rate figures stay stable. Judge frequency by list health numbers rather than a fixed rule of thumb. [L0603.023]
- Content created for the website, organic social, and retargeting can be reused in emails, which keeps frequency sustainable. Each email can deliver a useful piece from a growing content library. [L0603.011]

**Long nurture series**

- Long nurture series can be set to send daily for two months or a full year. A daily cadence is optional, not required for the flow to work. Choose the frequency based on how much the subscriber gains from each message. [L0572.023]

### Frequency Benchmarks and Limits

The sources give different figures. They do not state which applies in which situation, so each is listed with its context.

| Source | Figure | Context | Ids |
|---|---|---|---|
| Source E 2025-26 | At least 3 emails per week | Minimum for the weekly program | L0475.006 |
| Source E 2025-26 | About 3 per week; 2 per week is a decent baseline; 1 or 0 per month is far too low | Minimum target of 1 to 3 per week | L1235.021 |
| Source E 2025-26 | 3 to 5 emails per week | Manual campaigns, with new angles, problems, solutions, hooks, and trending content | L0494.005 |
| Source E 2025-26 | 2 to 4 times per month | Full subscriber list, including contacts who have not opened recently, as long as deliverability stays good | L0494.006 |
| Source A 2020 (older advice) | 2 emails per week | Target for subscribers; newly launched stores may send less due to resource constraints | L0245.039 |
| Source A | At least 2 emails per week | Lead list, usually one content email and one offer email, with holiday promotions added | L1076.013 |
| Source A | At least 2 per week, 1 per week minimum for offers | Promotional offer: one per week, year-round | L0240.004 |
| Source A | 2+ promotional emails per week | Separate from email funnels | L0226.031 |
| Source C | At least 1 per week | Normal store list | L1213.056 |
| Source C | 3-4 campaigns per month | Offer, event, new product, collection, giveaway, and deal-of-the-week campaigns | L0604.012 |
| Source C | About 4 campaigns per month | Includes a weekly 10% off deal plus one new product launch per month | L1213.056 |
| Source C | Core automations plus 1-2 campaign emails per month | Warning that more emails annoy customers | L1431.049 |
| Source B 2021-22 (older advice) | 5 per week | First 30 days: 4 automated plus 1 blast | L0603.008 |

- **Daily sending and revenue:** In one case, moving from one email per month to two per month more than doubled income. Daily emails were later found to be most profitable. A business that does not email its list daily can lose money every day. [L0523.127]
- **Raising frequency:** One operator who raised email frequency from once to twice per week saw email revenue rise by 15 to 17 percent, with no meaningful increase in complaints and little added cost. [L0942.025]
- **Messenger and email limit:** Limit messenger and email frequency to about one message a day, or one message every three days. Do not send too many messages in a row. Add an unsubscribe option, such as asking the customer to reply stop, in the first message. [L1243.044]
- **Frequency for the list:** Source A's list rules and Source E's send frequencies conflict only in scope: the Source E minimum applies to the weekly program, and the 2025-26 figures apply to the full list. [L0475.006, L0494.006]

### Content and Promotion Mix

**Split between brand and selling content** (Source E 2025-26)

- Split email content roughly 30/70. About 30% is brand-building, educational, and informative content that also entertains (infotainment), to nurture the audience and add value. About 70% is direct selling content. [L0475.002]
- Send the 30% brand-building content to every email segment, including recent buyers, because it builds the relationship without asking for a sale. [L0475.003]
- Do not send the 70% direct selling content to recent buyers, because they are not ready to buy again yet. [L0475.004]
- Quality comes from following the weekly mix. Brand-building, educating, entertaining, and nurturing keep building brand value and keep content fresh enough to open and click. The Problem-Solve format can always be used, because customers always have problems to solve. [L0475.015]

**Example content mix** (Source A 2020, older advice)

- Each email can combine four elements:
  - A link to a new blog post or content.
  - Niche-relevant news.
  - A product promotion, for example "buy this week save 2%".
  - A customer engagement request, for example "send us a photo of your product in your home". [L0245.040]
- Target email frequency is two emails per week, mixing value content (blog links, niche news) with promotional offers. [L0245.039]

**Offers and content per week**

- Release one promotional offer per week throughout the year via email to your lead list, to maintain engagement and convert prospects into customers. [L0240.004]
- Plan at least one relevant offer or piece of valuable content per week for every lead list, and ideally two. Waiting until the next holiday to email a new subscriber lets them forget the brand. [L1076.017]
- Broadcast offers to the list keep working for as long as subscribers remain on the list, but response rates are lower than the first follow-up emails after a purchase. Send them when a relevant new offer appears or during big sales seasons. [L0923.035]
- The job of email is to sell more of what already sells to people who do not yet own it. Top-selling products should therefore be the core of regular campaigns, including upsell pushes. [L0494.010]

**Content as a library**

- Every new piece of content should be added to the email program. A library of useful content gives the list something to read besides offers and promotions. Without content, email has little to say beyond product pitches, while content keeps subscribers engaged and warm between sales. [L0385.026]
- Email marketing is very hard to make effective without content. A subscriber list needs more than product offers to stay engaged. [L0500.008]
- Send interview or video content to the email list to add backstory, which can convince buyers who are still undecided. Email is for warming people already on the list. [L0941.078]
- A free weekly design sent by email keeps customers engaged while they wait for a physical product to ship. The emails also help the seller reach an engaged audience with high open rates and promote future offers. [L1153.065]

**Building and maintaining the list**

- Build and maintain the list in this order:
  1. Offer incentives (coupons, free accessories, giveaways) to collect emails.
  2. Send 2+ promotional emails per week, separate from email funnels.
  3. Include new blog posts, product announcements, promotions, and industry news.
  4. Monitor open rates and reply engagement.
  5. Periodically test affiliate partnership offers to monetize the list further. [L0226.031]
- Email marketing sends promotions to a broadcast list of subscribers to bring them back to buy again. Klaviyo can be used to build and manage this list. [L0597.012]

### Weekly Email Templates (Source E 2025-26)

**What's Hot Weekly (WHW)**

```
What's Hot Weekly (WHW) email layout, in this order:
1. Large hero image at the top.
2. Six-product block headed by the trending products of the last 7 days, showing 6 products.
3. Three customer testimonials.
4. Shop bestsellers section at the bottom.
Keep this layout as one reusable template and change only the products each week.
```

[L0475.007]

- Send the What's Hot Weekly email every Friday. Build it once as a template and resend it each Friday by swapping the products, which makes it easy to automate. [L0475.008]
- The six-product featured block can hold any of these:
  - The top six products by Shopify sales over the last 7 days.
  - The top six most-searched products.
  - Six products chosen manually.
  - A mix of these.
  - It can also feature a bundle or a deal of the week or month. [L0475.009]
- For a What's Hot Weekly campaign, place the selected products in the featured section at the top of the store, then move the other sections further down the page. The featured block should match the products in the email, so visitors who click through see the same offer. [L0475.016]

**Hot Tips (brand-building, part of the 30% share)**

```
Hot Tips email, a brand-building email (part of the 30% share): a headline announcing three tips, then three short numbered tips on a topic your customers care about, each with a brief explanation of how to apply it. Keep it free of a sales push.
```

[L0475.011]

**Problem-Solve (direct selling, part of the 70% share)**

1. Name one specific problem your target customer has.
2. Agitate it by showing the cost or frustration of leaving the problem unsolved.
3. Present your product as the solution to that problem. [L0475.012]

**Weekly schedule for 3 emails**

1. Monday or Tuesday: Problem-Solve email.
2. Wednesday or Thursday: Hot Tips email.
3. Friday: What's Hot Weekly email.
4. Optional: resend an email on Saturday or Sunday, but only with changed content. [L0475.013]

**Repeats and spam risk**

- Never resend the same content unchanged. When content matches what was recently sent, Google can route the email to spam. Change the content each time an email is repeated. [L0475.014]

**Campaign planning workflow** (Source E 2025-26)

- The campaign calendar is built in Google Sheets with Claude Cowork and the /email-strat-plan skill, using brand-kit.json and the audience segments as inputs. The output is a 30-day calendar with 4 campaigns, which are then fed into Klaviyo. [L1361.026]

### Newsletters and Content-Promotion Emails

**Publish first, then email**

- Publish the article first, then send the email to the list, so readers land on a live page. Repeat this cycle with every new keyword and keep a steady weekly rhythm. [L0380.029]

**Article email formula** (plain text, 4 to 6 sentences)

- Structure: open with a question or teaser, link to the article, repeat the same link once more, and end with a call to action such as checking out a new blog post.
- It works because a plain text message reads like a personal note, and short sentences move the reader quickly to the link. [L0380.030]
- Examples:
  - "Still unsure which hosting service fits a new podcast? I compared seven of them this week on price and ease of use. Read the full comparison here: [link]. Check the ranking and tell me which one you would pick. [link]"
  - "Still not sure a budget blender can handle frozen fruit? I tested four models for a month and noted what held up and what broke. See the results here: [link]. Let me know which one you would try first. [link]" [L0380.030]

**P.S. line**

- End the article email with a P.S. line that links to a second, related page, such as a product review. The main call to action stays the same. Readers who want more on the topic get one extra path toward a purchase, without a second competing message.
- Keep the P.S. to one sentence and one link. [L0380.034]

**Share requests**

1. When a new piece of content is published, send it to your entire email list.
2. Include past buyers, people who opted in through promotions on your site, exit-intent overlay sign-ups, and newsletter subscribers.
3. Ask every recipient to share the content with someone who would benefit from it. [L0899.003]
- Promotion emails should ask readers to share the content on Facebook, on X or Twitter, by forwarding it by email, or by embedding it on their own sites. The goal is for readers to consume the content and then pass it on, not just leave the site. [L0899.005]
- Sending content only once and not asking readers to share wastes most of the promotion potential. Content that is not worth sharing will not keep visitors on the page, so make it share-worthy first. [L0899.006]

### Reactive and Daily Story Emails

**Reactive campaigns**

- Campaigns are regular emails that are reactive, seasonal, trending, and relevant. Reactive marketing means turning news or trends in your target market into brand content quickly, sometimes the next day. [L0494.011]
- Reactive campaign formula: [relevant event] + [your product or angle] + [same-day or next-day send].
- When a news event, trend, or season hits your target market, send a campaign that ties it to your product within about a day. It works because the audience already pays attention to the event, so the email feels timely rather than like a generic promotion. [L0494.018]
- Examples:
  - Running shoes: a record marathon time makes headlines, so the next morning's email recommends a beginner shoe for people inspired to start running, with a 48-hour offer.
  - Coffee brand: a cold snap is forecast for the weekend, so the email promotes a hot-drink bundle sent Friday morning. [L0494.018]

**Daily story emails (Seinfeld-style)**

- After the soap sequence, Seinfeld-style emails are sent daily as broadcasts. They are usually entertaining stories from everyday life rather than pure content. Subscribers who finished the soap sequence move to a broadcast list where they receive that day's email. These emails are not part of a fixed auto-responder sequence. [L0523.125]
- Soap sequence emails run as an auto-responder with a fixed order. Daily Seinfeld emails are broadcasts that can be scheduled ahead but are usually tied to real events in the character's life. Keep the two systems separate in your email provider. [L0523.132]
- Shift the emails to roughly 90% entertainment and 10% content. In one case this increased opens, clicks, and sales significantly compared with a content-heavy approach. Entertainment is what keeps subscribers reading after they have bonded with the character. [L0523.126]
- Brainstorm topics by asking:
  1. What is happening in the character's life right now?
  2. What recently happened that was embarrassing?
  3. How are you getting through the holiday season?
  4. Where are you planning your next vacation?
  5. What did you recently buy that you regret?
  6. What did you recently buy that you adore?
  7. What made you furious yesterday that you laugh about today?
  8. What did a child or pet get into yesterday?
  9. What funny past event taught a lesson? [L0523.128]
- Every story must lead back to an offer, which can be your core product, another product of yours, or a partner's product. Entertaining emails that never connect to an offer will not earn money, however well they are written. The tie-in is what turns storytelling into sales. [L0523.129]
- Pattern for Seinfeld emails: a short everyday story, a turn that exposes a lesson or mistake, then a natural bridge to an offer. The subject line creates curiosity or a clear hook. The story keeps subscribers entertained every day, and the link makes every send commercially relevant. [L0523.130]
- Examples:

```
Example 1, subject '[True Story] I nearly lost a client over one spreadsheet'. Body: 'Yesterday a client called to say my report was wrong. I checked, and it was. The mistake came from a template I had trusted for years. That is why I now use the checklist in this guide: [link].'

Example 2, subject 'My dog ate the invoice (and what it taught me)'. Body: 'The dog chewed through the paper invoice this morning, which made me laugh. It also reminded me that I still send mine by post. The digital invoice tool I switched to last month is here: [link].'
```

[L0523.130]

- Copy the daily entertaining email onto your blog to create consistent content that leads readers toward a sale. The same story serves two purposes without extra writing. This is a quick way to keep the blog active. [L0523.131]

### Product Launch, Giveaway and Event Campaigns

**Campaign definition and ideas**

- Campaigns are one-time email blasts sent to a list or segment for events, launches, and seasonal offers, such as Valentine's Day, Father's Day, Mother's Day, Christmas, a new product launch, or early access for VIP customers. Unlike flows, they are not triggered by a visitor action. They are sent to an existing audience on a set date. [L0573.026, L0607.044]
- Campaign ideas for a store with a consistent seller: launches, offer launches, event launches (occasions such as Mother's Day or Valentine's Day), new product launches, specific collection promotions, bundle offers, giveaways and referral campaigns, and a weekly deal. Each should target a defined segment such as buyers, non-buyers, or VIPs. [L0605.049]
- Run 3-4 email campaigns a month from this list: offer launch, event launch, new product launch, specific collection promotion, giveaways, and deal of the week. [L0604.012]

**New product and category announcements**

- New product categories can be announced by email and SMS to existing customers, including the launch date and key features. Repeat purchases from these customers need no ad spend, so the sale is close to pure profit because the acquisition cost was already paid. Good customer service is a precondition for this to work. [L0434.007, L0435.006]
- Once you have a good customer email list, you can announce new products through an email campaign instead of paying for ads. This makes an existing customer base a low-cost channel for new product launches. [L1172.052]
- Email your customer list when you launch new products or promotions, such as a new private-label brand, because email drives traffic to products that Google Shopping rarely reaches. (Source A 2021, older advice) [L0968.068]

**New product launch sequence**

1. One week before launch, send a VIP sign-up email announcing the upcoming product with its features and images.
2. Offer VIP sign-ups an extra discount on launch, for example 30% off an $80 product.
3. Send three to four emails over the one-week period.
4. Count the sign-ups to estimate how many purchases to expect (for example 200 sign-ups out of 1,000 non-buyers).
5. Send the next email to the people who signed up, and a separate email to those who did not, asking why and offering an extra benefit point. [L0605.024]
- Campaigns should warm up the audience before asking for the purchase: educate first, then announce the launch, then ask for the sign-up. A direct buy request in the first email is too aggressive. [L0605.026]

**Weekly deal**

- Weekly deal campaigns have a comparatively low conversion rate, but they keep existing customers warm and prevent them from forgetting the brand after the first purchase. Combine a benefit of a new product with a small extra discount each week. [L0605.028]
- A weekly 10% off deal is part of a monthly structure of about four campaigns, plus one new product launch campaign per month. [L1213.056]

**Referral giveaway**

1. Run the giveaway on social media, asking followers to tag three friends and post to their stories.
2. In the email, ask the customer to refer three people using a referral or affiliate link.
3. Reward the referrer when a referred contact signs up with an email or phone number.
4. Add a larger reward when a referred contact buys. [L0605.050]

**Event teaser**

- Add an event teaser at the bottom of a regular content email:

```
PS: [Event name] is coming. [Link to signup page]
```

- The linked page should ask only for an email address to qualify interested buyers. [L0334.040]

**Creating and scheduling a campaign in Klaviyo**

1. Open Campaigns and click Create campaign. [L0607.045]
2. Enter a campaign name, for example the event and year, and set the start date (for example 15 January for a Valentine's Day push). Click Save and continue. [L0573.027, L0607.045]
3. Select the recipients: the Newsletter list or a segment such as VIP customers. Choose whether to exclude any groups. Choose whether to use tracking, and turn tracking on. [L0573.027, L0607.045]
4. Click Continue to content. [L0573.027, L0607.045]
5. Enter the subject line and preview text. [L0573.027, L0607.045]
6. Pick a template under Popular or from the template library. For an event, choose the event template from the Popular library. If there is no event template, change a similar template and edit the event name. [L0573.027, L0607.045]
7. Change the background color to white or your brand color, and add text, images, or a dynamic product section. [L0607.045]
8. Click Next. [L0573.027, L0607.045]
9. Turn Smart sending off. [L0573.027, L0573.028]
10. Choose Schedule or Send, then select Gradual send over several hours. [L0573.027, L0607.045]
11. Set the start time (for example 11 am Eastern) and the share of recipients per hour (for example 20 percent per hour). [L0573.027, L0607.045]
12. Click Schedule. [L0573.027, L0607.045]
