- Gradual campaign sending: send to a set share of recipients per hour, for example 20 percent per hour, instead of pushing all emails at one time. Turn Smart sending off for campaigns too. [L0573.028]
- Klaviyo is the email platform where offer emails are scheduled and sent to the broadcast audience. Scheduling of offers is done here alongside Google and Facebook. [L0757.032]

### Sponsored Blasts

- Sponsored email blasts: you pay to use a partner's email list to send your message. The price usually rises with the number of emails you send. (Source B, 2021-22, older advice) [L0525.013]
- A sponsorship does not have to be a single email. You can plan a series of emails with the partner that promote your products, drive visitors to your site, or point to your content. (Source B, 2021-22, older advice) [L0525.014]

## Holiday, Black Friday and Promotion Campaigns

### Q4 and Seasonal Planning

**Timing**
- Start preparing Q4 at least two months before the peak, and warm the email and SMS audience from about September or October. Interaction-based emails and surveys build the list before the sales period. [L1377.051]
- Use plain-text emails with one link or one form instead of heavy designs and images, because designed emails are more likely to land in the promotions tab. Surveys can be built with Jotform and sent to subscribers. [L1377.052]
- Plan the holiday content early. Steps:
  1. Create blog posts for major holidays (Black Friday, Cyber Monday, Christmas) 1-2 months before the event. [L0226.032]
  2. Write articles about upcoming sales, new inventory, and limited-time promotions. [L0226.032]
  3. Send an email to the list 2-4 weeks before the event with the article and promotional details. [L0226.032]
  4. Aim to rank in Google search when customers search "Black Friday [niche]" or "Cyber Monday deals." [L0226.032]
- Run a campaign for every major event or occasion, such as Christmas or Black Friday, because customers expect a discount at these times. This adds to the regular weekly and monthly campaigns. [L1213.057]
- Use seasonal events such as Valentine's Day for promotions and extra emails. Plan promotions and emails around holidays to create reasons to send more messages. [L0934.060]
- Plan a monthly promotion to the email list and build it into the regular calendar. Monthly promotions lift sales and help the store scale into other platforms later. [L0941.079]
- Follow up holiday promotions in a later month. A promotion for a holiday can close sales that a first visit did not convert. [L1325.024]
- Match email promotions with the same offer on Meta at the same time. Past customers who bought months ago, such as six months back, should receive both the email and the Facebook promotion. [L0649.024]

**Event campaigns**
- If Klaviyo's template library has no template for a seasonal event, pick a template for a similar occasion and adapt it, for example a New Year layout for Valentine's Day. Replace the headline, offer text, and background color so the email matches the event. [L0573.035]
- A seasonal gift promotion, such as a Father's Day offer, fits stores whose products appeal to dads. Promote it with a short series of emails to the subscriber list. [L0857.071]
- An event-based promotion sent to the email list, such as 5% or 10% off on May 5 for Cinco de Mayo, is a quick way to earn extra sales. (Older, 2019.) [L1002.038]
- Event campaign for occasions such as Mother's Day or Valentine's Day, in this order:
  1. Choose a specific marketing angle for the event. [L0605.027]
  2. Send the first email to educate the audience and announce the upcoming offer, without a hard pitch. [L0605.027]
  3. Send the next email with a benefit of the product and a sign-up request. [L0605.027]
  4. Count the sign-ups to estimate the sales. [L0605.027]
  5. Send the follow-up to sign-ups, and a separate email to non-sign-ups asking why they did not sign up, with an extra benefit point. [L0605.027]

**Monthly promotion procedure (older, 2022)**
1. Choose a high-ticket product from a supplier with good margins. [L0963.137]
2. Send an email offering a discount on that supplier's products, for example 10 percent off. [L0963.137]
3. Link directly to the product collection. [L0963.137]

**Email volume: sources disagree**

| Source | Guidance | Ids |
|---|---|---|
| 2019 (older) | During the holiday sale, send about three emails per week: one content email and two promotion emails. Send promotions to the best-selling products and the special offer page, and start holiday emails right away. | L0467.061 |
| 2019 (older) | Before Black Friday and Cyber Monday, set up holiday sequences in Klaviyo that send coupon codes and special offers, often almost every day through the season, to customers and to site opt-ins. | L1110.032 |
| 2020 (older) | Volume should be tiered: one major promotion early, a reminder closer to the holiday, and no flood of messages. Adjust the schedule based on past campaign results. | L0468.135 |
| 2021 (older) | No universal sequence fits every store. The number of emails depends on the offer, where the list came from, and how often you contact subscribers. | L1112.028 |
| 2024 | For each holiday promotion, plan one email per promotion day, and use the same tone and offer in emails and ads. | L1115.079 |
| 2024 | A Q4 email plan can include a couple dozen emails over about three months, so plan the copy for the whole season early. | L1115.104 |
| 2024 | A five-promotion US Q4 plan totals about 17 emails: 4 for Halloween, 5 for Black Friday with Thanksgiving, 2 for Cyber Monday, 3 for Christmas and 3 for New Year's. | L1285.101 |

**Black Friday and Cyber Monday announcements (older, 2019)**
- Send an email to your list, post on social media, and use every channel you have to announce a Black Friday sale and a Cyber Monday sale. Even a small email list or a small social following can produce extra sales from these promotions. [L1086.046]
- In 2019, Black Friday fell on November 29 and Cyber Monday on December 2. Plan holiday promotions around these dates in the year they apply. [L1086.047]

**Abandoned cart contacts (2023)**
- Abandoned cart contacts who are not subscribed to the main list can receive about one promotional email per month, tied to a holiday or niche event. [L1094.103]

### List Building Before the Peak

- Build an email and SMS list before the Q4 peak, because acquiring customers gets expensive then. Start retargeting ads after roughly two to three weeks of traffic, once you have stats to target from. [L0692.040]
- List building avoids high CPMs. During the holiday season, CPM and CPC spike and CTR drops on all ad platforms (Facebook, Google, Pinterest, TikTok). Instead of direct sales ads, send traffic to a landing page that collects email addresses, which builds a qualified audience for engagement and future sales. [L0307.001]
- Email marketing is a free or low-cost channel during BFCM and delivers strong ROI. Prioritize email list building and VIP segmentation before peak season. [L0298.051]
- Build the email list from organic and paid visitors, since subscribers can be promoted to during monthly promotions. Sync the monthly promotions with your ad accounts so buyers see them on social too. [L0941.077]
- Holiday promotion emails should go to the existing list regardless of its size, whether it has around 50 or around 50,000 contacts. Each email should include the discount code and aim to bring back visitors who have not decided to buy. [L0623.005]
- A small email list is still worth using for Q4 promotions. The remarketing budget for a small audience is about $5 a day, and the sales are close to pure profit. [L1285.097]
- A small email list still makes the holiday sends worth doing, because the remarketing spend with a small audience costs about $5 per day, and the sales it brings are close to pure profit. A few responses out of 100 to 300 contacts across five promotions justify the work. [L1223.020]
- Set up abandoned cart emails, a content opt-in capture, and promotional follow-ups before a high-traffic sales period. This lets you contact visitors who did not buy on their first visit. (Older, 2018.) [L1274.010]
- Black Friday and Cyber Monday results depend heavily on remarketing to the audience built up during the year, not only on first-time visitors. Give that audience a reason to return with a promotion. (Older, 2018.) [L1274.011]
- Expect slow sales from influencer digital products. Collect emails for one to two months while keeping the audience engaged with the influencer, then blast the promotion to the list and on stories to create strong sales days. [L0789.039]
- Case: A store spent about $50,000 on Facebook ads in Q4 but got most of its sales through its email and SMS marketing. It warmed its list from October with surveys, plain-text emails and a launch of one or two offers per month, then sent VIP bundle offers before the sales period. Retargeting campaigns with Black Friday and Cyber Monday offers reached roughly 8x ROAS in some campaigns. [L1377.054]

### Dated Promotion Calendars

**Black Friday and Cyber Monday sequence (Source C)**
1. November 24 (48 hours before launch): send "Going live in 48 hours. Last year everything sold out in 4 hours. You're among our first 100 subscribers, so take advantage." Include SMS. [L0307.005]
2. November 25 (24 hours before): send "Going live in 24 hours." Tease a product image with a blurred price (e.g., show "9.99" to create curiosity). Include SMS. [L0307.005]
3. November 26 (Black Friday, launch day): send the launch email and SMS with a link to the hidden category page. [L0307.005]
4. November 27 (day after Black Friday): send "We had huge success, received many requests, some items sold out. We just restocked limited pieces. Shop now." [L0307.005]
5. November 28 (Sunday): send "This is your last day. Only a few items left. Shop now." Send SMS. [L0307.005]
6. November 29 (day after): send "It's finished. Thank you for your trust and business." Send SMS. [L0307.005]
7. Monday (Cyber Monday, one week later): launch a new offer with a different discount structure (example: change from 50% off to 40% off plus a 50% cashback voucher for the next purchase). Send the new offer email and SMS, then a 48-hour countdown, then a last-chance email, then a closure email. [L0307.005]
8. Optional Black Week extension (November 29-30, only 2 days): send an email plus SMS, then a UGC email, then a closure email "It's over." [L0307.005]

**Black Friday sequence for a store**
1. Before the sale, send an email announcing Black Friday week and asking customers to decide now what they want. [L1363.057]
2. On the day, send the main announcement with the bigger discount. [L1363.057]
3. Retarget visitors who did not buy with a message saying they missed the offer. [L1363.057]
4. After the sale, send an email saying the sale has been extended for people who missed it. [L1363.057]

**Hidden Black Friday category page**
- Create a product category page that is not linked in website navigation or menus. It is only accessible via direct links from email, SMS, or retargeting ads. Include all Black Friday-discounted products, a countdown timer showing the promotion end time, and low-stock quantity labels on each product to create urgency. [L0307.006]

**Welcome series and standard flows during BFCM**
- Standard email flows (abandoned cart sequences, welcome series, post-purchase) remain active and run in parallel during Black Friday promotional campaigns. Do not pause them. They operate on their own triggers and segments, separate from the VIP Black Friday list. [L0308.035]
- For ongoing welcome series, if new leads enter after Day 3 emails have started, pause emails 2 and 3 for those new subscribers to prevent clashes with Black Friday email blasts, which have priority. Resume the welcome series after the promotion period ends. [L0307.004]
- The welcome series runs automatically on fixed dates (e.g., Nov 1-3) regardless of when individual subscribers join. For subscribers entering late (e.g., Nov 22), pause emails 2 and 3 to avoid overlap with high-priority Black Friday blasts. Resume normal sequences after promotions end. [L0307.019]
- Reconciling the two: standard flows stay active [L0308.035], and the welcome-series pause applies only to late joiners' emails 2 and 3 during the promotion period [L0307.004, L0307.019].

**Campaigns versus flows**
- When time is short, use campaigns instead of flows for Black Friday emails. Campaigns are simpler: send an initial email to the list, analyze results, then send a new campaign to the list minus unsubscribes. There is no need for conditional flows. [L0308.007]

**Q4 2024 US holiday calendar (2024 dates)**
- Halloween (Thursday 31 October):
  1. Send emails on 29 October and 30 October. [L1285.087]
  2. Send an email on Halloween, 31 October. [L1285.087]
  3. Send an extension email on Friday 1 November saying the offer runs one more day. Pair this with Meta and Google remarketing ads. [L1285.087]
- Black Friday and Thanksgiving:
  1. Send one email each on Monday 25 November, Tuesday 26 November, Wednesday 27 November, Thursday 28 November (Thanksgiving, themed around the holiday with the coupon), and Friday 29 November (Black Friday, when the sale closes). [L1285.088]
  2. Run remarketing ads synced with these emails. [L1285.088]
- Cyber Monday (2 December, a one-day promotion):
  1. Send one email early in the morning on 2 December. [L1285.089]
  2. Send a second email around 5 PM the same day saying the sale is about to end. [L1285.089]
  3. Run remarketing ads alongside. [L1285.089]
- Christmas:
  1. Send emails on Monday 9 December, Wednesday 11 December, and Friday 13 December. [L1285.090]
  2. Make the message that this is the last time to buy and still get the item delivered before Christmas, since about two weeks of delivery time remain. [L1285.090]
- New Year:
  1. Start the campaign on 30 December. [L1285.091]
  2. Send an email on New Year's Day, 1 January. [L1285.091]
  3. Send a final email on Friday 3 January, when the offer closes. [L1285.091]
  4. Run remarketing ads alongside. [L1285.091]
- Each promotion uses its own email count and send dates from the calendar. Send the planned emails on dated sends, and extend the offer with one more email the day after the event when needed, as with Halloween's extra email on 11/1. [L1221.003]
- Send one email per day with a distinct angle rather than repeating the same message. Each email should carry the same offer and coupon code so customers can act on any of them. [L1105.007]

**Christmas promotion (older, 2020)**
1. Launch the holiday promotion email as early as possible, ideally right away. [L0468.134]
2. Include the message that products arrive before Christmas if ordered now. [L0468.134]
3. Send one or two reminder emails as the holiday gets closer. [L0468.134]
4. Do not send the main holiday promotion on the holiday itself, because most customers have already bought. [L0468.134]

**Five-day holiday promotion**
1. Send an email at the start of the promotion announcing the sale and including the discount code. [L0623.006]
2. Send an email in the middle of the window, or on a following day, saying the sale was extended to the final day. [L0623.006]
3. Send a last email stating that the code expires at midnight on the final day. [L0623.006]
4. Every email should include the code. [L0623.006]
- For a five-day promotion, spread a three-email sequence across the window: the first email on the launch day, the second about two days later, and the third on the final day. Each email should match the stage of the promotion it is sent in. [L0620.004]

**One-week December promotion**
- For a one-week December promotion with three emails, send the gain email on the first day, the logic email two days later, and the fear email on the last day. For example, December 8, December 10, and December 12. [L1224.019]

**High-ticket promotion calendar**
- Close offers on a weekday. For more expensive, high-ticket products, the offer should close on a Friday. [L1205.022]

**Timers and extensions**
- Klaviyo email campaigns can include countdown timers that are reset in each send. When a sale is extended, update the timer and the matching offer text in every campaign so the messages stay consistent. [L1225.087]

**Repeat buyers**
- Email customers who bought a single item with a targeted discount to buy a gift or a second item for a partner or family member. This extends the value of first-time buyers in the holiday season. [L1225.010]

### Promotion Segments and Exclusions

**Standard promotion segment (2024-2025 rule)**
- For a promotion email, send to all contacts in the list, except people who purchased in the last 30 days and contacts currently in an evergreen automation, such as the abandoned cart flow. This works with either Shopify Email or Klaviyo. [L1221.005, L1222.007, L1223.018, L1285.064, L1285.105, L1287.045]
- Segment settings:
  - Include: all contacts.
  - Exclude: contacts who purchased in the past 30 days.
  - Exclude: contacts currently in the abandoned cart flow (or any evergreen automation).
  - Segment size does not matter; a list of 500 or 10,000 contacts uses the same rules. [L1223.018]
- Promotional emails go to all contacts except two groups: contacts who bought in the last 30 days, and contacts currently inside an evergreen automation. [L1221.005]
- Recent buyers and automated flows should not receive the promotion sequence. [L1222.007]

**Exclusion window: sources disagree**

| Source | Rule | Applies to | Ids |
|---|---|---|---|
| 2021 (older) | Exclude recent buyers for 30 to 90 days, depending on the promotion and store type. | Choose the window per promotion. A window of about 30 days is typical for most promotions. | L0469.100 |
| 2024 | Exclude contacts who bought in the last 30 days. | Current standard for Shopify Email and Klaviyo segments. | L1221.005, L1223.018, L1285.064, L1285.105 |
| 2025 | Exclude contacts who purchased in the last 30 days. | Current standard. | L1222.007, L1287.045 |

**Abandoned cart contacts: sources disagree**
- 2021 (older): keep sending promotions to subscribers who have not bought yet, including opt-ins and abandoned cart contacts, and to past buyers outside the exclusion window. Most people on a growing list are not yet customers. [L0469.101]
- 2024 and 2025: exclude contacts currently in the abandoned cart flow or any evergreen automation. [L1223.018, L1285.105, L1285.064, L1287.045]
- Apply the newer rule to segments built today. The 2021 advice still describes the non-buyer audience that a growing list contains. [L0469.101, L1223.018]

**Tools for segments and sends**
- Shopify Email or Klaviyo can run the holiday promotions. Only one of the two is needed. Either tool handles the segments and the scheduled sends for the Q4 campaign, and the same tool must handle the promotional sends. [L1223.017, L1221.004]

**Matching timer and offer rules**
- Each flow needs one reliable discount code, and the expiry you promise in the copy must match the expiry configured in the store. [L0606.013]

### VIP Early Access

**BFCM VIP early access email (Source C)**

```
BFCM VIP Early Access Email: Subject emphasizes exclusivity (e.g., 'Your 24-Hour VIP Early Access Starts Now'). Body: Explain that subscribers get 24-hour first access to upcoming BFCM offers; this creates urgency and FOMO without requiring truly different pricing-simply send the email 24 hours before the public campaign launches. Landing: Direct to a dedicated BFCM collection or campaign page. Effect: Drives eager subscribers to site before the main sale begins.
```
[L0298.049]

**VIP positioning for lead capture**
- For Black Friday lead capture in Facebook Creator Studio, use a VIP positioning: "Enter details to get exclusive discounts up to 60% off with early access." This entices email submission without over-promising. [L0308.005]

**VIP access sequence (Source E 2025-26)**
1. Start VIP access at least 72 hours before the sale begins. [L1225.007]
2. Give VIP access first to ambassadors. [L1225.007]
3. Then open it to organic followers. [L1225.007]
4. Then open it to the email list. [L1225.007]
- Do not run VIP access paid for 30 days. [L1225.007]

**Trade-off of early access**
- Replacing a welcome discount pop-up with an early-access sign-up usually softens sales in the short term. The trade-off is a larger list of interested buyers that converts strongly once the sale starts, so the fair value exchange must be clear to the subscriber. [L1225.008]

**VIP pre-Black Friday email sequence (Source C)**
1. Email 1 (day of signup): "Welcome to the Club." [L0308.008]
2. Email 2 (2 days before): "What you're going to Choose" (explore products). [L0308.008]
3. Email 3 (day before): "It's around the corner." [L0308.008]
4. Email 4 (day of): "Here's your exclusive early access." [L0308.008]
5. Mention in the final email that the discount releases to the public tomorrow, so act now. [L0308.008]
6. Final email on Black Friday: "Here we go, buy now." [L0308.008]

**Private sale and list-building ad copy**

```
[EVENT NAME] Private Sale
Get [DISCOUNT]% off [PRODUCT CATEGORY]
Exclusive for Members
To get exclusive early access:
1. Click the button below
2. Complete the form
3. Get your [DISCOUNT]% discount code
Spots are limited. [SCARCITY LINE, e.g., 'Only 100 spots available.']
Complete your details now →
[BUTTON TEXT: e.g., 'Claim My Discount']
```
[L0307.016]

**VIP sign-up campaign with bundles**

```
Before Black Friday and Cyber Monday, run a VIP sign-up campaign by email and SMS for existing buyers and subscribers. Offer an extra 20 to 30 percent discount on bundle offers, such as a complete kit, launched during the sales period. Bundles tend to perform well in that period, so frame the VIP offer around them.
```
[L1377.053]

### Gain, Logic, Fear Sequences

**Order and purpose**
- Each promotion's email sequence follows the order Gain, Logic, Fear: first the benefit, then the rational reason to act, then the fear of missing out. The series is meant to move the reader through different emotions that lead to a purchase. [L1222.005]
- The three-email sequence can follow the gain, logic, and fear structure: the first email shows the benefit, the second gives the reason it works, and the third shows the cost of not acting. The structure is an older copywriting formula, and AI tools can help adapt it to an e-commerce store. [L0857.072]

**Gain-logic-fear sequence (2024 pattern)**
- Email 1 states what the buyer gains (the benefit plus the offer). Email 2 gives a rational reason the offer is the smart choice. Email 3 says the offer is ending now. The sequence moves the reader from desire to justification to urgency, which is why it converts consistently across promotion types. [L1224.018]
  - Example A (kitchen knives, Black Friday): "Get 20% off the premium knife set, delivered before the holiday" / "Why one quality knife set replaces five cheap ones" / "Final hours: the 20% code ends at midnight". [L1224.018]
  - Example B (pet beds, Cyber Monday): "Cyber Monday: 15% off orthopedic pet beds with fast shipping" / "Why a supportive bed reduces night-time restlessness for older dogs" / "Last chance: the 15% code expires tonight". [L1224.018]

**Formula with examples (2025)**
- A three-email promotion sequence follows the gain, logic, fear pattern. Email one states what the buyer gains, email two explains why this offer is a sensible choice, and email three says the offer is ending, so the timing creates urgency and the sequence works across most promotion types. [L1287.047]
  - Example A: Gain, 15 percent off plus delivery before the holiday weekend. Logic, a gift that arrives on time saves a last-minute store trip. Fear, the 15 percent code expires Sunday at midnight. [L1287.047]
  - Example B: Gain, a free carry strap with every yoga mat ordered this week. Logic, the buyer avoids a shopping trip to a sports store. Fear, the top color is limited and the strap offer ends Friday. [L1287.047]

**Closing-email patterns**
- Closing-reminder email: the subject line says the discount ends at a specific time, and the body gives the code and a reason to act today. Reuse the same subject line as the headline of the remarketing ads. Example new subject lines: "Last Call: 20% Off Ends Tonight" and "Final Hours to Lock In Your Holiday Price". [L1223.021]
- Buy-by-date email: the message states the last order date that still guarantees delivery before a holiday. The reason is the delivery window, not the discount. Example new subject lines: "Order by Dec 13 to Get It Before Christmas" and "Your Last Chance to Ship Before the Holidays". [L1223.022]

**Remarketing during an open window (high-ticket)**
1. Send one pre-promotion email before a high-ticket offer starts. [L1205.025]
2. Send one email per day during the promotion window. [L1205.025]
3. On the closing day, send at least two reminder emails. [L1205.025]
4. Run Facebook and Google remarketing ads to the same people who receive the emails, for maximum reach. [L1205.025]

**Remarketing timing**
- Start retargeting ads after roughly two to three weeks of traffic, once you have stats to target from. [L0692.040]
- During an open promotion window, run remarketing alongside the emails, as in the procedure above. [L1205.025]

### Subject Lines by Holiday

**Formula and structure**
- Holiday subject line formula: event name or seasonal cue, then a concrete savings promise, then an optional emoji or short action word. The event cue shows why the email matters now, and the savings promise gives a reason to open it. Examples in the same pattern: "Spring Clearance Starts Today: Save on Your Favorites" and "Summer Sale Is Live, Up to 30% Off Our Picks". [L0547.004]
- Write a separate set of subject lines for each seasonal moment (Halloween, Black Friday, Cyber Monday, Christmas, New Year) instead of reusing one generic sale line. Each moment has its own vocabulary and should be matched to its own campaign. [L0548.001]
- Across a holiday campaign, mix three types of subject lines: direct deal statements, urgency lines, and mood or holiday lines. Varying the type keeps recipients from tuning out a repeated message format. [L0548.011]
- Use one emoji per subject line as an accent, placed at the start or end, and choose it to match the season (ghost, cart, gift, fire, laptop). Skip emojis when the brand or audience does not suit them. [L0548.010]

**Subject line patterns by holiday**
- Halloween: combine a spooky or playful theme (ghosts, treats versus tricks, monster-style wordplay) with a savings promise, often with one ghost or pumpkin emoji. The theme makes the offer feel fun and fits the season, so the discount reads as a treat. Examples: "Spooky Season Steals: Up to 30% Off Everything 🎃" and "Witch Better Believe These Deals Are Here 🧙". [L0548.002]
- Black Friday: use the words "Black Friday" with a savings or deal promise and a sense of now, such as early access, the biggest sale, or a last chance. Exclusivity ("just for you", "early access") and time-bound language make the email feel important. Examples: "Black Friday Early Access: 25% Off Starts Right Now" and "Last Chance: Black Friday Prices End Tonight 🛍️". [L0548.003]
- Cyber Monday: name the day and pair it with either a countdown of hours left or a sitewide percentage off, with "Extended" used when the sale runs longer. The online focus (laptop or phone emoji, "sitewide") signals that the whole store is in the sale. Examples: "Cyber Monday: 12 Hours Left for 40% Off Sitewide 💻" and "Cyber Monday Extended: Our Deals Stay Live Through Tonight". [L0548.004]
- Christmas: blend holiday warmth (gifts, Santa, decorations, festive phrasing) with a savings message and a deadline-style phrase such as "before they're gone" or "countdown". The festive tone builds gift-buying urgency. Examples: "Santa's Gift List Is Ready: Save 20% on Top Picks 🎁" and "Christmas Countdown: Grab Your Favorites Before Stock Runs Out 🎄". [L0548.005]
- New Year: frame the offer as a fresh start, using resolutions, "new year, new" phrasing, or "start the year" wording plus a savings promise. This ties the purchase to a personal goal, which makes the offer feel purposeful. Examples: "New Year, Better Savings: Start 2027 With 15% Off 🎉" and "Your Resolution Starts Here: Save Big on Your Favorite Brands". [L0548.006]

**Placeholders and dates**
- Fill every placeholder before sending: X%, XX% and similar must become the real discount, NAME must become the merge field of your email tool, and any year must be the current year. A leftover placeholder in a live email is a visible error that damages trust. [L0547.005]
- Use placeholders in subject lines for the discount percentage (X%) and the year (20XX), and for personalization use a NAME field that is filled per recipient. Replace every placeholder with the real figure before the email is sent. [L0548.008]
- Do not leave an old year in a seasonal subject line, such as an outdated "Welcome 20XX" phrase from a previous year. Update the year in every subject line for the current season before sending. [L0548.009]

**Reuse and extensions**
- Do not reuse the same subject line across different events or repeat an identical line in the next campaign. Tie wording to the specific offer of each event so recipients do not see the same message twice. [L0547.008]
- When a sale is extended, send a follow-up with "Extended" wording instead of repeating the original subject line. The change signals a new reason to open the email and keeps the offer credible. [L0548.012]

**Ready-made list**
- When AI-written email headlines are weak, use a proven list of holiday subject lines grouped by holiday (about 97 lines covering Halloween, Black Friday, Cyber Monday, Christmas, and New Year's). Use them as email subject lines or ad headlines. [L1224.020, L1287.054]

### Scarcity, Urgency and Teasers

**Urgency must be real**
- Urgency must be real. Valid reasons include a webinar starting tomorrow, a limited number of seats, a limited stock of a physical product, or a video being taken offline. For evergreen products, use a special sale that ends soon or a coupon that expires in 24 hours. [L0523.120]
- Fake urgency backfires and destroys credibility once subscribers notice. Invented deadlines teach readers to ignore future emails. Only use deadlines you can honor. [L0523.121]
- Do not claim urgency that is not real. Lines such as "only 4 hours to go", "last day", or "extended" must match the actual sale timer or end date. Use "before stock runs out" only when inventory is truly limited, because subscribers who find a fake deadline stop trusting later messages. [L0547.006, L0548.007]
- Urgency phrases such as "hours left", "ends tonight", or "last day" should only be used when the deadline is real. False countdowns damage trust and make later emails less effective. [L0548.007, L0547.006]

**Deadlines and expiry**
- Within each flow, every later email shortens the deadline: 3 days, then 48 hours, then 24 hours or a few hours. The repeated shrinking window creates escalating urgency without changing the offer itself. Examples: "Offer ends in 72 hours" followed by "Offer ends in 24 hours"; "Your code expires this weekend" followed by "Last chance: expires at midnight tonight". [L0606.017]
- Set the send delays so that each email arrives while its deadline statement is still true. The lesson does not give fixed delays, so the timing must be derived from the deadlines in the copy. [L0606.015]
- If an email says the offer ends at midnight, name the time zone and make the coupon expire at exactly that time. A midnight claim that differs from the real expiry damages trust the same way a false stock claim does. [L0606.041]

**Stock claims**
- Scarcity claims such as "selling out like crazy", "inventory is running low", or "we might not be able to fulfill your order" must be true for the store at the time of sending. False stock claims damage trust and can create refund and complaint problems. [L0606.019]
- Use scarcity messaging in Black Friday campaigns, such as: "This exclusive discount releases to the public tomorrow. Act now to lock in the early-bird price before the public gets access." This creates FOMO without over-exaggerating the offer. [L0308.031]

**Teasers**
- Teasing a larger promotion:
  1. Send a teaser email to the broadcast list a few days before the offer starts, for example on the Friday before a Monday start. [L1204.046]
  2. Hint at the offer without giving the full details, so people start thinking about the purchase. [L1204.046]
  3. Send the full offer at the start of the window. [L1204.046]
- Show a product image with a blurred price a day before launch to create curiosity (e.g., "9.99"). [L0307.005]
- For high-ticket offers, send a single pre-promotion email before the offer starts, saying a big offer is coming and asking the audience to check their inbox first thing on the start day at a specific time. Example: for leather sofas averaging $2,500, send the email the Friday before a Monday-to-Friday promotion. This prepares buyers for a larger purchase. [L1205.021, L1205.030]

### AI Prompts for Sequences

**Building the prompt first (procedure)**
1. Write one message that includes the store domain, promotion dates, discount code, percentage off, delivery guarantee, and the send date of each email. [L1224.036]
2. Ask the AI to first write the best prompt for this task instead of asking for the emails directly. [L1224.036]
3. Review and edit that generated prompt for your own store. [L1224.036]
4. Send the edited prompt, then ask for the sequence using the gain-logic-fear structure. [L1224.036]
5. Use a fresh, customized prompt for each promotion rather than one template for all of them. [L1224.036]

**Remarketing and sequence build (procedure)**
1. Set the promotion's start date, end date, discount, and discount code. [L1286.005]
2. Add a delivery guarantee if the promotion has a deadline such as Christmas. [L1286.005]
3. Write a 3-email sequence and send one email on the first promotion day, one in the middle, and one on the last promotion day (for a Monday to Friday window: Monday, Wednesday, Friday). [L1286.005]
4. Write remarketing ad copy for Meta and Google Display based on the same email copy and offer details. [L1286.005]

**Prompts**

```
Hi, my store is at [your store URL], and I'm planning a [promotion name] running from [start day, date] to [end day, date]. Shoppers can enter the code "[discount code]" to get [discount %] off everything, and orders are guaranteed to arrive before [delivery deadline]. Please draft a three-email sequence: email 1 goes out on [date of email 1], email 2 on [date of email 2], and email 3 on [date of email 3]. First, give me the strongest prompt I could use to get the best possible results. For each email, include a subject line, preview text, and the goal of that email. The discount code, discount percentage, and delivery promise must stay exactly the same in all three emails.
```
[L0620.001]

```
Hello, I run [STORE DOMAIN] and am planning a [HOLIDAY] sale from [START DATE] to [END DATE]. Shoppers can enter code [CODE] to save [X]% on everything, and orders are promised to arrive by [DELIVERY DEADLINE]. Please draft a three-message email series: the first goes out on [DATE 1], the second on [DATE 2], and the third on [DATE 3]. Before you write the emails, give me the strongest prompt I could use to get the best possible result.
```
[L1224.016]

```
Build a three-email [HOLIDAY] promo sequence for [STORE NAME] using the gain logic fear framework. Each email should connect to the campaign running from [START DATE] to [END DATE], featuring the [X]% discount code [CODE] and guaranteed [DELIVERY] delivery. Base the sequence on the prompt you produced most recently, applying the gain logic fear framework.
```
[L1224.017]

```
Hi, I run [STORE NAME], which lives at [STORE URL]. From [START DATE] to [END DATE], I'm hosting a [PROMOTION NAME]. Shoppers can enter the code [DISCOUNT CODE] to get [DISCOUNT] off, and the offer is backed by a [DELIVERY PROMISE, e.g. delivery before Christmas Day] guarantee. Please draft a three-part email series: the first goes out on [DATE 1], the second on [DATE 2], and the third on [DATE 3]. Before you write the emails, give me the strongest prompt I could use to get the best possible output.
```

Please create the email sequence based on your latest prompt, using the gain, logic, fear framework. Each email must be tied to its send date within [PROMOTION DATES], to the discount code [DISCOUNT CODE] giving [DISCOUNT] off, and to the guarantee of [DELIVERY PROMISE]. Write a three-part promotional email sequence for [STORE NAME] for the [PROMOTION NAME], with the first email presenting the gain, the second presenting the logic and the third presenting the fear, with a subject line and a call to action for each email.
```

Great, thanks! Here's the plan: we're running a three-day Halloween sale at our eCommerce store, __________ (insert your store name). It starts October 29, 2024 and ends October 31, 2024, with one email going out on each of those days, all offering 10% off the whole site. Write 3 distinct emails that include the coupon code "happyhalloween" and invite readers to visit the store to use the deal. Make them funny and appealing to anyone shopping for a new __________ (insert your product category). Each email needs a subject line under 50 characters, a short preview text, and one clear call-to-action button label. Keep every email under 150 words, and make sure each one shows the coupon code and the offer (10% off sitewide, valid October 29-31, 2024).

First, give me the strongest prompt I could use to get the best possible output. Then, for every email, supply a subject line, preview text, body copy, and a call to action. ## Testing, Measurement and Optimization

### Where to Test: Campaigns or Flows

- Run major tests in email campaigns rather than flows. A campaign sends one variant to a large list at once, which produces the volume needed for a reliable decision. Campaign tests also target differences large enough to show up statistically. [L0570.006]
- Typical differences worth testing in a campaign, each comparing two versions of one element: [L0570.007]
  - CTA button color change [L0570.007]
  - Lifestyle image versus product shot [L0570.007]
  - Benefit-driven headline versus curiosity-driven headline [L0570.007]
- Low-volume flows, such as abandoned checkout, can take many months before any decision is possible. Validate ideas in campaigns first, then move them into flows. [L0570.013]
- Abandoned-cart sequences can be split-tested with different angles and hooks even on a low-traffic store. Keep the flow structure that works and vary the messaging. [L1208.021]

| Test location | Volume and time needed | Units |
|---|---|---|
| Campaign to a large list | 20,000 contacts at a 50/50 split gives 10,000 per variant. A 50,000 to 100,000-contact list can reach roughly 50,000 per test in one send. | [L0570.009] |
| Abandoned-checkout flow | About 1,000 per variant can take about six months, depending on the brand. Some brands never reach that volume. Around 2,000 abandoned carts in a test can take a full year. | [L0570.004] |
| Welcome series | Very large volumes are needed, so results are slow. Plan for long test durations before acting on any result. | [L0570.005] |

### Test Design and Sequencing

- Clone a working campaign, change a few elements such as the subject line or the offer, and send the copy to a different list. Comparing the two campaigns shows which version performs better. [L0514.020]
- Use the following sequence to move from campaign tests to flows: [L0570.010]
  1. Run the split test as a campaign to a large list (for example 20,000 to 100,000 contacts) to reach a decisive sample size.
  2. Choose the winning version and use it as the basis for the next campaign.
  3. Keep changing one proven element at a time and resending, so the template improves with each send.
  4. Once the template reliably generates revenue on every send, treat it as the proven template.
  5. Implement the proven winning ideas in automated flows and run the flow tests as A/B tests.
  6. Because the ideas were already validated at scale, flow tests with lower volume are more likely to produce a winner.
- Repeatedly sending a winning template keeps it as the baseline for future tests, which reduces guesswork. Flow changes then rest on campaign data rather than assumptions or long waiting periods. [L0570.011]
- Almost every flow element, including timing, subject lines and offers, must be tested in your own account with your own customers. Treat any source that presents email flow advice as absolute rules with skepticism. [L0588.005]
- Small-batch variant testing, a direct-mail procedure: [L1261.156]
  1. Write several letter variants with different headlines and offers.
  2. Mail each variant to a small batch of prospects, such as 1,000 prospects per variant across 25 variants.
  3. Measure cost per response or per customer for each variant.
  4. Use only the best-performing letter for the full mailing.

### Baselines and Changelog Tests

- Log every test in a changelog with its date, the change made, the expected result, the baseline, and the result. Record the result even if it is entered after the test end date. [L0410.007]
- Worked example, a sender profile picture test on Shopify email:
  - Change: on 15 November, the sender profile picture was replaced with an animated GIF instead of a static image. [L0410.007]
  - Expected result: open rate up by 5 percentage points. [L0410.007]
  - Baseline: 90-day trailing open rate of 45%, so the target was an open rate above 50%. [L0410.007]
  - Record whether the open rate stayed at 45%, rose to 50%, or rose further. [L0410.007]
- The sender profile picture is the small avatar that Gmail shows next to the sender name in the inbox. Replacing a static avatar with an animated GIF is a test you can run on Shopify email to see whether it moves the open rate. [L0410.016]
- The two sources for the same test state the goal differently. Use the one that matches how you write your target: [L0410.007, L1509.010]

| Reading of the goal | Open rate target from a 45% baseline | Units |
|---|---|---|
| +5 percentage points (absolute) | 50% | [L0410.007] |
| +5% (relative increase) | About 47.25% | [L1509.010] |

- Baselines by test: [L1509.009, L1509.010]

| Test | Judged by | 90-day trailing baseline | Units |
|---|---|---|---|
| Percentage-off opt-in incentive replaced with a quiz | Revenue per opt-in | $80 per opt-in | [L1509.009] |
| Sender profile picture changed to an animated GIF | Open rate | 45% | [L1509.010] |

### Format and Copy Tests (Older Advice, 2021-22)

- Test plain text against HTML formatting with your own audience and market, and measure click-through rate as the main metric. Keep whichever format performs better, since there are very few universal truths in email marketing. [L0602.020]
- Email performance improves through continuous sending and measuring results over time. Refine your brand's tone and offer style through testing, since each market responds differently. [L0609.004]
- When email traffic or email sales are weak, review the emails before changing the audience. Check the following, then test other angles: [L1456.037]
  - Whether the right emails go to the right people
  - Whether the message fits
  - Whether recipients are engaged enough to buy
  - Whether they are at the right stage of their buying journey

### Frequency and Engagement Metrics

- Test frequency over time to find the point where results plateau. Track four metrics to decide whether the frequency is right: [L0603.012]
  - Unsubscribes
  - Open rate
  - Click-through rate
  - Conversion rate
- Test each segment's performance using audience breakdowns to see which recency windows and engagement thresholds convert best. Then adjust future sends accordingly. [L0264.012]
- Review campaign performance by audience breakdown on contact age (days engaged buckets such as 0-14, 15-30, 31-60 and 31-90), not only by overall open rate. [L0864.001]
- A steady decline across older buckets is an early sign of deliverability or list-quality problems. [L0864.001]
- Example of open rate by bucket: [L0864.002]

| Days engaged | Open rate (example) |
|---|---|
| 0-14 | About 60% |
| 15-30 | About 53% |
| 31-60 | About 41% |
| 31-90 | Dropped further (no value given) |

- The sharpest drop between buckets matters more than the absolute value. [L0864.002]

### Reading Results by Inbox Provider and Domain

- Campaign performance is found under Campaigns > [campaign] > Deliverability. It shows Performance by inbox provider and Performance by domain. [L0864.003]
- Audience breakdown, with an Export CSV option, sits next to it under Audience breakdown. The campaign list shows Open rate, Click rate, Successfully Paid and Revenue columns. [L0864.003]

### Judging Campaigns After the Full Send

- Do not judge a campaign while it is still sending. Campaigns in progress show queued recipients (for example 1,018 still queued), so wait until the send has finished before reading the open rate. [L0864.015]
- Campaigns can be scheduled to send at each recipient's local time, so the send is spread across several hours. Judge results only after the full send window has passed. [L0864.016]

### Attribution and Reporting

- Use a different discount code per flow so you can see which flow produces the sales. Otherwise cart recovery and welcome offers cannot be separated in reporting. [L0606.014]
- The Shopify Messaging dashboard shows, for a chosen date range: [L1317.004]
  - Number of emails sent
  - Email open rate
  - Conversion rate
  - Number of sales generated by the emails

### Flow Audits

- The audit and reporting stage needs at least 30 days of Klaviyo send data, read through the Klaviyo MCP. It produces an audit report and a reporting calendar, and /email-draft-review supports the review. [L1361.023]
- Klaviyo's preview does not make discount links clickable, so you cannot test them from there. Copy the real link and open it in a customer-style browser session to confirm the code applies at checkout. [L1470.011]

### Claims to Treat with Care

- A weekly-design newsletter reported open rates above 70%. The seller called this a strong engagement signal for upselling. It describes one newsletter and is not a general target. [L1153.066]

### Common Mistakes

- Testing directly in low-volume flows, such as abandoned checkout, can take many months before any decision is possible. [L0570.013]
- Judging a campaign while it is still sending, before queued recipients have been delivered. [L0864.015]
- Testing discount links in the preview, which does not make them clickable. [L1470.011]
- Accepting flow advice as absolute rules instead of testing it in your own account. [L0588.005]

## Outreach and Supplier Email

### Mailbox setup and profile photo

- Send outreach from a personal address: either a real name at your own domain, or a new personal mailbox on that domain. Generic addresses such as contact@, info@ or support@ read as spam and lower the reply and link rate. *(older, 2021-22)* [L0574.002]
- A profile photo on the sending Google account appears in recipients' inboxes next to every email. A real face makes outreach feel personal and tends to raise the chance of a positive reply, whether it is your own face or someone else's. *(older, 2021-22)* [L0574.003]
- If the profile photo upload fails, follow this procedure:
  1. Open your Google account, click the profile picture, choose a photo from your computer, crop it and set it as profile photo.
  2. If the upload fails, open the Google Admin console and type 'profile edit' into the search bar, or go to Apps > Additional Google Services > Directory settings, then open Profile editing.
  3. Tick the Photo option ('Users can customise their public profile photo and cover photo').
  4. Click Save.
  5. Go back to the Admin console home page, wait, and try the photo upload again.
  6. If it still fails with an error code, leave it and retry later, because changes can take up to 24 hours to propagate to all users.

  *(older, 2021-22)* [L0574.004]

### Warm-up limits

- Sending a large batch of cold emails from a mailbox that normally sends little can make Google suspect unusual behaviour, and it may suspend the account (usually temporarily). Warm the mailbox up by raising the daily sending volume gradually over time rather than jumping straight to hundreds per day. *(older, 2021-22)* [L0574.016]
- Warm-up ramp for a new outreach mailbox or domain: *(older, 2021-22)* [L0574.017]

  | Phase | Timing (as taught) | Max emails per day |
  |---|---|---|
  | Start | First campaigns | 20 |
  | Step 2 | After about 2 weeks | 50 |
  | Step 3 | After several more weeks | 100 |
  | Ceiling | Keep increasing over time | up to 300 |

  Mailshake spreads the batch across days up to the limit, so a large list does not need to go out in one day. *(older, 2021-22)* [L0574.017]
- Start every new campaign with a sending limit of 20 emails per day. A large list can take a week or more to finish, and that is expected. *(older, 2021-22)* [L0946.033]
- A steady trickle of responses is easier to manage than a single large batch sent at once. Spreading sends over days also protects deliverability and keeps the inbox manageable. *(older, 2021-22)* [L0946.034]

### Signature and unsubscribe handling

- Use this signature for every outreach email from the connected mailbox. Insert the link on the words 'click here' with the unsubscribe placeholder described in the setting below. Keep the signature simple, with no large fonts, bright colours or many links. *(older, 2021-22)* [L0574.019]

  ```
  [Your name]
  [Company name]
  [Phone number]
  If you'd like to stop receiving emails from us, click here to unsubscribe.
  ```

- Keep the email signature short and plain. Mailshake advises avoiding large font sizes, colours and images, not adding too many links, and starting with a blank line. A signature may be left empty if you prefer. *(older, 2021-22)* [L0574.020]
- Add the unsubscribe link to the signature by selecting the words 'click here' and inserting a link. Use {{unsubscribe_url}} as the URL and do not add an http prefix. This placeholder lets Mailshake add the recipient to an unsubscribed list and automatically stop future emails, including follow-ups. *(older, 2021-22)* [L0574.021]
- An unsubscribe link in every outreach email gives recipients a way to opt out, so they are less likely to mark the email as spam. Including it by default in the signature means it is never forgotten in a template. A visible opt-out is considered common practice and a courtesy. *(older, 2021-22)* [L0574.022]
- Mailchimp handles opt-outs automatically when a campaign contains its special unsubscribe link, and it compiles a list of contacts who unsubscribed. Outside Mailchimp, the Mailshake {{unsubscribe_url}} placeholder fills the same role for the sending tool. *(older, 2021-22)* [L0574.031]
- In Mailshake, the left sidebar has an Unsubscribes section that lists contacts who clicked the unsubscribe link. Check it before reusing a contact list, so that people who opted out are not emailed again, including in follow-ups. *(older, 2021-22)* [L0574.032]
- If you receive emails from a business you are already enrolled with, the signup email may differ from the one on your email list. Send an unsubscribe request from the address that receives the emails, stating that you are already a member. *(older, 2019)* [L0960.067]

### Cold outreach sequence

- Scope: cold email outreach to business decision makers is not part of this approach, so do not use it to sell to B2B buyers. Reach trade customers through a dedicated trade page with a contact form and a phone number instead. *(older, 2020)* [L1088.096]
- Cold outreach emails must be very short, simple and straight to the point, because a stranger gives only a few seconds of attention. State the exact request in the first email and avoid long explanations. *(older, 2021-22)* [L0946.001]
- Build a three-email outreach sequence in this order: *(older, 2021-22)* [L0946.002]
  1. Initial email: makes the first request and is the most detailed of the three.
  2. Follow-up #1: a short reminder that refers back to the first email.
  3. Follow-up #2: a final, short message that closes the loop.

  Send all three, because many responses come only after the follow-ups. *(older, 2021-22)* [L0946.002]
- Always follow up on any outreach or sales request, because most positive responses come from follow-ups. Mailshake can automate the follow-up sends once they are added to the campaign. *(older, 2021-22)* [L0946.003]
- Wait 5 days between the initial email and each follow-up, and between follow-up #1 and follow-up #2. Keep the default reply-chain setting unchanged so follow-ups appear as replies in the recipient's inbox. *(older, 2021-22)* [L0946.004]
- In the Mailshake campaign composer, follow-ups are added with ADD FOLLOW-UP, next to the ADD DRIP and ADD ON CLICK options. Keep the default five-day wait between follow-ups and the default reply-chain setting. Use the recipient counter, such as 1 of 35 recipients, to step through each personalized email before clicking NEXT, because edits are saved when you switch recipients or click NEXT. *(older, 2021-22)* [L0946.039]

### Subject lines

- Keep subject lines simple and generic so they do not trigger spam filters. Use 'you' and questions to feel personal, but do not mention links, content, outreach or collaboration in the subject, because that makes people skip the email. *(older, 2021-22)* [L0946.005]
- Subject-line formula: a short, personal question or open phrase that creates curiosity without revealing the request. It works because it looks like a human note, avoids spam trigger words and gets the email opened, after which the body makes the ask. Examples in this pattern: 'Would this fit your post on {{Topic}}?' and 'Any thoughts on an idea for your guide?' *(older, 2021-22)* [L0946.006]
- Each follow-up subject line starts with 're:' followed by the original subject line. This keeps all three emails in one visible thread for the recipient. *(older, 2021-22)* [L0946.007]
- Putting links, outreach words or product names in the subject line makes recipients skip the email. Also avoid spammy wording in the subject line, because it can send the email to spam. *(older, 2021-22)* [L0946.036]

### Outreach templates

- Ready-to-send opening email. Leave every double-brace field as it is, because Mailshake fills them with data from the spreadsheet. Swap each square-bracket prompt for your own words and remove the prompt itself. Mailshake adds the signature automatically. *(older, 2021-22)* [L0946.008]

  ```
  Subject: [Write your subject line following the subject-line pattern]

  Hi {{Firstname/[Add a creative fallback here]}},

  My name is [Name] and I write for [website], a site that [describe your site in one line].

  I'm getting in touch about your article at {{Prospect URL}} [explain why a link to us is worth their time]

  [Ask them to look at your link]

  [Insert your link here]

  [Ask them for feedback]

  Thanks,
  [Name]
  ```

- In the introduction, state what the site helps people do in one line, and name the specific article that was found using the prospect URL field. Then ask for a link and a reason why it is worth the recipient's time in the same message. *(older, 2021-22)* [L0946.014]
- Ready-to-send first follow-up. Leave the double-brace field as it is and replace each square-bracket prompt with your own words. The subject line is 're:' followed by the original subject. *(older, 2021-22)* [L0946.009]

  ```
  Subject: re: [Original subject line]

  Hi {{name/creative fallback}},

  [Introduce yourself again: your name and the site you write for]

  [Ask whether they've had a chance to read my earlier email]

  Thanks.
  ```

- Paste-ready template for follow-up #2, the final message. Keep the double-brace field unchanged and replace each square-bracket instruction with your own text. Use the subject line 're:' followed by the original subject. *(older, 2021-22)* [L0946.010]

  ```
  Subject: re: [Original subject line]

  Hey {{name/creative fallback}},

  [Remind them who you are and what you are asking them to look at]

  [Ask if they got the chance to check out your proposal]

  [State that this is the last message and that you will leave it there if they are not interested]

  Thanks.
  ```

- Final follow-up formula: state clearly that this is the last message and that you will leave it there if they are not interested. It works because it respects the recipient's time, reduces unsubscribes and closes the thread politely. Examples in this pattern: 'This is my last note, so I will leave it there if this is not a fit.' and 'Last check-in from me. If the idea is not for your site, no problem at all.' *(older, 2021-22)* [L0946.035]
- Early follow-up email template for a contact who asked to talk by phone. Use it after a call, and keep the text as written:

  ```
  Today's discussion was valuable. I want to confirm it covered everything I need from you. Here are my contact details. Thank you for your time, and I'm eager to begin our working relationship.
  ```

  *(older, 2021-22)* [L0235.005]

### Supplier follow-up

- Use an automated email sequence that sends follow-up questions after each reply, which shows which suppliers respond. Use it to get responsive suppliers to reply in English. [L0832.034]
- If a supplier asks you to send an email after a phone call, send the day one email from the tool. You can copy it directly and edit it before sending. [L0936.009]
- If the day one email gets no reply, send the day two follow-up email. It refers to your earlier message, states the launch timing and asks for the application steps. [L0936.010]
- Formula for a supplier follow-up email: refer to your earlier message, state a near-term launch window, commit to paying by credit card and following the supplier's pricing rules, then ask for the application steps. It works because it reminds the supplier of your request, signals you are a low-risk new account and makes the next reply easy. [L0936.011]
  - Example 1: 'Following up on my note from last week. We launch in the coming months and want to present your brand properly. We will pay by card and follow your pricing policy. Could you send me the application steps?'
  - Example 2: 'Checking in on my email from Monday. Our store opens within a few weeks and we plan to represent your products carefully. We will pay by credit card and respect your listed prices. What are the next steps to apply?'

### Composer fallbacks, checks and campaign settings

- Mailshake fallback: if the recipient's first name cannot be found, the fallback text entered in the highlighted field is shown instead of the field name. Write a neutral or creative fallback for every name placeholder, because a literal field name looks broken. Fallbacks are never perfect, but they look better than an empty or raw field. *(older, 2021-22)* [L0946.011]
- The sender name in the email must match the name in the body, and the sending address must match the website named in the introduction. Use only one from-address per campaign, and keep the signature personal. *(older, 2021-22)* [L0946.012]
- Before clicking Next in the composer, check that every text replacement field exists for all recipients. Mailshake warns when some recipients are missing a field. Use only fields that all recipients have, or add a fallback. *(older, 2021-22)* [L0946.030]
- Review each email before sending: *(older, 2021-22)* [L0946.031]
  1. Use the preview to read the subject, the body and the signature, including the unsubscribe line that Mailshake adds.
  2. Confirm the email sounds like a human, the signature is correct and the call to action is clear.
  3. Send a test email and check it on a phone.
  4. Click Next.
- Campaign options in Mailshake: enable Track opens and Track link clicks if you want the data, use Schedule this send to pick a start time in the local time zone, and leave Lead Catcher enabled so replies are captured. Accept the terms about anti-spam laws and the important notes, then click Next to start. *(older, 2021-22)* [L0946.032]
- Mailshake's composer can save a written email as a reusable template with SAVE AS TEMPLATE, and CHOOSE TEMPLATE loads saved ones, so a template library builds up inside the account. Use SEND TEST before launching to receive the email yourself and check it. *(older, 2021-22)* [L0946.038]
- When text pasted into the Mailshake composer carries colours or other formatting, Mailshake may offer to auto clean the formatting. You can decline that offer and then check the preview, which shows the subject, body and signature as the recipient will see them. *(older, 2021-22)* [L0946.040]

### Pitfalls

- Deleting or renaming the double-brace or bracketed fields breaks personalization or leaves raw field names in the email. Only change the square-bracket instructions and the text around them. *(older, 2021-22)* [L0946.029]
- Long, detailed outreach emails reduce replies because recipients give only brief attention. Keep the request in the first two or three lines. *(older, 2021-22)* [L0946.037]

### Customer support email

- Customer support email should be answered within 24 hours, Monday through Friday. *(older, 2021)* [L1090.116]
- Customer emails can be answered any day of the week, but phone calls to customers should not be made on Sundays. *(older, 2021)* [L1090.115]
- Contact-form and store inbox messages before a purchase are sales leads and should be treated as valuable, possibly more valuable than a paid click. Questions about options or features indicate demand, and repeated requests can be a signal to add that feature. [L0910.016]
- When building an AI Workflow to generate automatic email replies, add as much context as possible to the system prompt: details about the recipient, your preferred response tone, how you typically handle similar requests, any specific business information the AI should reference, and any guidelines about what topics you do or do not discuss. Rich context in the prompt dramatically improves the relevance and quality of generated replies. [L0287.021]

## Other details

### Inbox and team tools

- In Gmail, create filter rules that send unwanted email straight into a folder, so spam does not clutter your inbox every time you log in. [L1285.103]
- To share a Google Docs or Google Sheets file for a team to copy, set the share link to view-only, so anyone with the link can read it. The recipient then opens File > Make a copy to save their own editable version in their Google Drive. [L1223.035]

### Building and owning audiences

- The email list and the lists of followers and engagers are the assets used to attract partners and sales opportunities. A brand with an engaged list can do business with related companies easily. [L0487.058]
- Pinterest with a linked store and product pins costs nothing to set up, but it is not a major source of sales compared with Google and Microsoft. [L0855.155]
- Instagram Broadcast Channels suit brands with an already engaged audience, and a minimum follower count may be required before a channel can be created. Check whether the format fits the page before investing time in it. [L0576.018]
- Broadcast Channels work like a VIP club: followers feel they get behind-the-scenes access. The brand can post one-way, run quick polls and ask for future video ideas, while followers can answer but cannot freely send messages to the whole group. [L0576.019]
- Broadcast messages are a one-way stream, so encourage emoji reactions on them rather than expecting full replies. Reactions are a low-effort way for followers to feel connected to the brand. [L0576.017]
- Livecom's channel page with the stored RCPs is segmented inside Klaviyo, so the streams can be promoted to those segments. Videos embedded in the bubbles can also be reused in Klaviyo email campaigns. [L0951.010]
- Messenger broadcasts were presented as having higher open and click rates than email, because most people already have Messenger on their phone. The source figures were shown only as a screenshot, so the exact percentages are not stated in the lesson. [L1243.019]

### Affiliate follow-up emails

- Affiliate follow-up emails serve two purposes: building trust through helpful content, screenshots and case studies, and asking the reader to take a specific action. Emails that only give content and never ask for anything usually produce no sales. [L0514.019]
- Use this follow-up template:

```
Subject: Quick check-in

Hi {first_name},

How is your progress going? I wanted to see if you have started making money online yet. If you have not, here is the resource I recommend to start: {affiliate_link}

It helped me, and I can personally vouch for it. Let me know if you have any questions.

P.S. If you have not started yet, {affiliate_link} is the best place to begin.
```
[L0514.021]

- Place the affiliate link at the end of follow-up emails, usually in a P.S. line, and link to the specific sales page. Owning a subscriber's email gives you a long-term contact until they unsubscribe, so the content must stay valuable enough that they keep opening. [L0514.022]

### Collecting extra shipping charges after the order

*Older advice (2020).*

- To collect expedited or upgraded shipping charges after the order, create a draft order in Shopify or a PayPal invoice, then send it to the customer for the additional payment. [L0268.055]

### Subscriber opt-out and re-subscription

*Older advice (2023), not marked as older by the 2019-2022 rule.*

- Opting out of a single email from a membership or course list can stop all further emails from that list. To receive updates again, give written permission to the support team so they can re-add the address. [L0804.110]

### Product title tags

- Add the words "buy" and "online" to product page title tags, as long as the store already has some traffic momentum. Expect roughly 10-30% more traffic and a larger sales lift. The typical template is "Buy [Product Name] Online", followed by the brand name if it fits. Some CMSs cannot do that order, so use "[Product Name] Online" instead. [L0575.001]
- Add buy/online to product title tags in one of three ways: [L0575.002]
  1. Manually edit each product if you have few products (about 10 products in 15 minutes).
  2. On larger CMS stores, use an SEO plugin with a title and meta description template for product pages.
  3. For thousands of products, export to CSV, use formulas in Excel to build the new titles in spare columns, then import the file back.

### Sales claims (not practice rules)

*Older material (2021-22). This is sales copy from a course's own offer, not a practice rule. Do not copy its claims or its urgency tactics into your own store.*

- A Dropship Breakthrough offer was presented with a 42% discount from a $4,997 annual price to $2,987 annual, or a monthly option of $9.97 for three months, with a 72-hour deadline. The stated 42% does not match the prices: $2,987 is about 40% below $4,997. [L0414.046]
